Categorical quantum symmetries and ribbon tensor 2-categories
Hank Chen

Abstract
In a companion work on the combinatorial quantization of 4d 2-Chern-Simons theory, the author has constructed the Hopf category of quantum 2-gauge transformations C ~ = 𝕌 q ​ 𝔊 \tilde{C}=\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G} acting on the discrete surface-holonomy configurations on a lattice. We prove in this article that the 2- 𝖧𝗂𝗅𝖻 \mathsf{Hilb} -enriched 2-representation 2-category 2 ​ R ​ e ​ p ⁡ ( C ~ ) \operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C}) of finite semisimple ℂ \mathbb{C} -linear C ~ \tilde{C} -module categories is braided, planar-pivotal, and lax rigid, hence 2 ​ R ​ e ​ p ⁡ ( C ~ ) \operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C}) provides an example of a ribbon tensor 2-category . We explicitly construct the ribbon balancing functors, and exhibit their coherence conditions against the rigid dagger structures. This allows one to refine the various notions of framing in a 2-category with duals that have been previously studied in the literature. Following the 2-tangle hypothesis of Baez-Langford, framed invariants of 2-tangles can then be constructed from ribbon 2-functors into 2 ​ R ​ e ​ p ⁡ ( C ~ ) \operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C}) , analogous to the definition of decorated ribbon graphs in the Reshetikhin-Turaev construction. We will also prove that, in the classical limit q → 1 q\rightarrow 1 , the 2-category 2 ​ R ​ e ​ p ⁡ ( 𝕌 q = 1 ​ 𝔊 ) \operatorname{2Rep}(\mathbb{U}_{q=1}\mathfrak{G}) becomes strict pivotal in the sense of Douglas-Reutter.



Contents

1
Introduction

Over the past century, the algebraic structures describing the symmetries of various physical systems of interest were found to be captured by
quantum groups
. Examples include the XXX/XXY/XYZ family of integrable spin chains
[
Jon90
]
and the Wilson loop observables of 3-dimensional Chern-Simons theory
[
Wit83
]
.
Compact
quantum groups
[
Maj11
,
Wor88
]
, in particular, such as the quantum enveloping algebra
U_{q}\mathfrak{g}
of Drinfel’d-Jimbo
[
Dri86
,
Jim85
]
associated a semisimple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, play a very important role in these physical examples. These ideas were then extended substantially over the past few decades to construct various types of topological quantum field theories (TQFTs). Furthermore, quantum group symmetry can also be found
[
AFS92
,
KL94
]
hidden within the algebra of current operators of the conformal field theory living on the
boundary
of such 3-dimensional TQFTs.

Subsequently, it was discovered that the representation theory of quantum group Hopf algebras was able to give rise to invariants of 3-manifolds
[
RT90
,
TUR92
,
BJ12
,
BW96
,
Res10
]
. This jump-started the field of
quantum topology
, in which methods of theoretical physics — namely field theory and gauge theory — was applied to study the topology of 3-manifolds. The celebrated
Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFT
can be thought of as the cornerstone of the entire field. One of, if not
the
, reason that quantum groups and their representations turned out to play such a crucial role in the topology of 3-manifolds is that (i) Hopf algebra representations encode algebraically the properties of
(1-)tangles
in 3-space, and (ii) the geometry of 1-tangles encode the topology of 3-manifolds.

To be more precise, 1-tangles are collections of copies of the interval
[0,1]
embedded into a slice
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,1]
, such that their endpoints are located at
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0,1\}
. The ambient isotopies of these 1-tangles fixing their endpoints are well-known to be generated by the so-called
Reidemeister moves
. A link (ie. a collection fo embedded copies of
S^{1}
) can then be obtained by attaching the top and the bottom endpoints of a 1-tangle. It was in fact known that invariants of links and knots can be obtained from Hopf algebra quantum groups
[
Jon90
]
, prior to the construction of quantum 3-manifold invariants.

A classic theorem of Lickorish-Wallace
[
Lic62
,
Wal60
]
states that any closed compact 3-manifold can be obtained from the 3-sphere
S^{3}
by a procedure known as
surgery theory
. Briefly, one can take an embedded framed link in
S^{3}
, excise its tubular neighborhood, perform a so-called "Dehn filling" on the excised torii, then glue it back to obtain another 3-manifold. The link equivalences under which the resulting 3-manifolds are diffeomorphic are known as the
Kirby moves

[
Kir78
]
. These two aspects of 3-manifold topology were combined in the seminal work of Reshetikhin and Turaev
[
RT90
,
RT91
]
, where quantum invariants of 3-manifolds were obtained by decorating the surgery links in
S^{3}
with the data of a ribbon tensor category, such as
\operatorname{Rep}(U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
, the representation category of the quantum enveloping algebra.

Now over the past decade, significant efforts have been dedicated to investigating the higher-dimensional analogue of the above phenomenon — a "4-dimensional categorified quantum topology" of sorts. The success of the cobordism hypothesis
[
BD95
,
Lur08
]
of Baez-Dolan to classify higher-dimensional TQFTs led many to explore the homotopy properties of higher-dimensional analogues of tangles — the so-called "2-tangles" — through higher categorical algebras
[
BL03
]
. On the other hand, several 4-dimensional field/gauge theories were constructed throughout the late 20th to early 21st century, which gave rise to very interesting invariants that can detect exotic smooth structures. Examples include (but may not be limited to) the Donaldson invariant
[
Don90
]
, the Seiberg-Witten invariant
[
Moo01
]
, the Rozansky-Khovanov homology
[
MWW19
]
, and the Kontsevich integral
[
CD01
]
. However, aside from the Khovanov homology story (see
[
MWW22
]
), it is not yet clear if these are related to the homotopy theory of 2-tangles.

In order to complete the analogy with the Chern-Simons/Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFT, the author has opted to begin from the perspective of higher-gauge theory. Based on the theory of derived
L_{n}
-algebras and their associated
non-Abelian
bundle gerbes (with connection)
[
Bae+12
,
Woc11
,
NW11
,
Sch11
,
KS20
]
, one can develop the so-called "homotopy Maurer-Cartan theories"
[
Jur+19
]
, which can be understood as higher-dimensional generalizations of Chern-Simons theory in the derived context. At dimension 4, in particular, the homotopy "2-Chern-Simons" theory has relatively recently received attention in both the context of physics and mathematics
[
SZ14
,
Zuc21
,
CL24
,
SV24
]
. Particularly in the companion work
[
Che25
]
by the author, a framework for the combinatorial quantization of this 4d 2-Chern-Simons theory was developed on a lattice, in which the analytic and Hopf categorical structures of the underlying surface holonomy degrees-of-freedom were unraveled.

This paper is dedicated to the detailed categorical study of the (finite semisimple linear) 2-representations of the categorical gauge symmetries
\tilde{C}
in 2-Chern-Simons theory. Physically, they correspond to (a local algebraic description of) the Wilson loop and surface observables (see
[
KT17
,
Che23
]
), and mathematically they form a 2-category denoted by
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
. One of the results in
[
Che25
]
proves that
\tilde{C}
is a
Hopf monoidal category
(cf.
[
CF94
,
DS97
,
BCV16
]
), and
\tilde{R}
is a so-called "cobraiding" which behaves like a categorical version of a quantum 2-
R
-matrix. More details can be found in §
2
.

In this paper, we shall focus entirely on the braiding, adjunctions, and duals of
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
. We will describe all of the coherence conditions of the
ribbon balancings
, such that a notion of a "ribbon 2-functor" can be understood as a 2-functor between two rigid dagger braided tensor 2-categories preserving these ribbon balancings. By leveraging the 2-tangle 2-category of Baez-Langford
[
BL03
]

\mathcal{T}
, the notion of
decorated ribbon 2-tangles
can then be defined as a ribbon 2-functor

\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R}).

Indeed, the 2-tangle hypothesis then dictates that such a functor would determine the quantum 2-Chern-Simons theory as a functorial 4d TQFT, whose quantum invariant on a closed 4-manifold can in principle be constructed through a 4-dimensional version of the Reshetikhin-Turaev functor.

1.1
Summary of results

We begin by mentioning some previous works in the literature which sought to capture the geometry of 2-tangles using 2-categorical notions. We will also refer to places in this paper where generalizations and refinements of these results can be found.

1.

The earliest work on this, to the best of the author’s knowledge, is
[
BL03
]
. In this paper, a "braided 2-category with duals" is introduced, which serves to algebraically model the geometric and homotopical aspects of tangles embedded in 4-space. This 2-tangle 2-category also has a single self-dual generator, which is "unframed" in the sense that it has equipped a trivialization of the first Reidemeister move. As we will discuss in §
5.1.2
, the notion of duality suffices for unframed objects, but not in general. We will explain the situation of
[
BL03
]
in the context of our paper in §
6.1.4
.

2.

In the seminal work of Douglas-Reutter
[
DR18
]
, they introduced the notion of semisimple and fusion 2-categories, as well as, subsequently, pivotality and sphericality. In defining pivotality, two distinct notions of duality and rigidity were introduced: planar-rigidity and object-level rigidity. The object-level dual was defined to be strictly involutive/reflexive, which allowed a certain pivotal condition to be imposed. In §
4
, we identify this particular pivotal condition as the main culprit for the drawback of their framework mentioned in Warning 2.2.5 in
[
DR18
]
. We will demonstrate in §
6.1
how the
ribbon balancing
underlying our 2-category can resolve this issue, and "unstrictify" the pivotality of Douglas-Reutter to a notion that may be called "
SO(3)
-volutvity"
[
Fer+24
]
.

3.

Over the past decade, the properties of a
\mathsf{Gray}
-categories equipped with duals had been under study
[
BMS24
]
. The author believes that this framework is the closest one to this paper, due to the fact that
\mathsf{Gray}
-categories with duals are the natural algebraic description of the local part of non-extended 3d defect TQFTs
[
CMS20
]
. We shall see in §
5.1
and §
6.1.3
how many of the structures appearing
[
BMS24
]
) also appears in
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
.

Much of the writing of this paper have taken significant inspiration from the above cited papers, and we will make references to them frequently whenever appropriate. The "main result" here, so to speak, is the definition of a more refined notion of "framing" for objects in a braided 2-category in §
6.1.3
.

fully-framed

half-framed

unframed

self-dual

\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})

(or its delooping)

ribbon tensor

2-category

\mathsf{Gray}
-category

with duals

[
BMS24
]

(with monoidal product)

pivotal 2-category

[
DR18
]

(with braiding)

2-tangle 2-category

with one self-dual generator

[
BL03
]

Table 1:
A schematic table displaying the various notions of "framed-ness" in
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
and how they relate to existing structures that have already appeared in the literature.

We show that there are in fact
four
levels of "framed-ness" (see tables
1
and
2
), each of which correspond to data that trivializes the duality and adjunction structures to a certain degree. We will in particular note in
Remark
6.5
how being "half-framed" is closely related to the structures studied in
[
BMS24
]
.

A synoptic diagram organizing all of the 2-categorical structures involved in this paper are exhibited below (as inspired by
[
Jam16
]
).

where the arrows
\rightarrow
are forgetful functors and
Z_{1}
denotes taking the Drinfel’d centre. The various refined notions of "framing" described in Table
1
can be attributed to the interaction of the ribbon balancing
\vartheta
with
lax
structures of rigidity.

The new insight here is that all of these geometric structures, including the old known ones (eg. planar-pivotality
Theorem
3.5
and "2-category with duals"
Theorem
5.1
) as well as the new ones (eg. the ribbon balancing structures §
6.1
and the higher-Hopf links §
6.2
), were extracted from studying properties of
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
— namely the observables in quantum 2-Chern-Simons theory. Though not completely general, this perspective has the advantage that it allowed us to pinpoint exactly when the 2-category
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
has, for instance,

•

not just braided/
E_{2}
-structure but a
sylleptic
/
E_{3}
-structure (see
Remark
5.1
), and

•

a braiding of finite-order (see
Remark
6.6
).

Moreover, we will show in §
7
that in the undeformed classical limit, we recover
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C}\mid_{q=1};\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{id})
as a symmetric (namely
E_{4}=E_{\infty}
) 2-category equipped with a pivotal structure in the sense of
[
DR18
]
. The triviality of the
quadruple object-level dual
will be proven by the author and collaborators in a soon upcoming work (see also
Remark
6.3
).

1.2
Overview

We will begin in §
2
with a concise review of what we mean by a "Hopf category
\tilde{C}
", and how such an algebraic structure appear in the quantum symmetries of 2-Chern-Simons theory
[
Che25
]
. It models a categorical version of the quantum enveloping algebra. Then, in §
3
, we will use its Hopf categorical structures to determine the braided monoidal structures of the 2-category
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
of its finite semisimple linear 2-representations, by leveraging previous works
[
Neu97
,
Gur11
,
KTZ20
,
CG23
]
.

Then, the strategy is as follows:

1.

We introduce the adjoints and duals in §
3.2
and §
3.3
, respectively, then we study their mutual compatibility in §
4
. This led us to the notion of a "rigid dagger tensor 2-category", and we use this structure in §
4.2
to unveil the main cause of the issue behind
Warning 2.2.5
of
[
DR18
]
.

2.

Then, in §
5
and §
5.2
, we include the braiding into the discussion. By examining the planar-unitarity of the braiding, we recover the notion of "braided 2-category with duals" described in
[
BL03
]
, as well as its
writhing
and the
fold-crossings
coherence 2-morphisms
[
BL03
,
CRS97
]
.

These data and properties make
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
into a
ribbon tensor 2-category
. In §
6.1
, we introduce the ribbon balancing from the above braided rigid structure. These ribbon balancings are used to define various notions of "framing" of an object, organized in table
2
. We showed how these notions of framing reduce to those described in previous literature, as listed in table
1
.

Next, in §
6.2
, we studied and constructed the
Hopf link
functors. Some were found to be trivializable, and we describe the coherence conditions they satisfy. Further, we also list in table
3
the different types of Hopf links that one can form depending on the framing.

Finally, in §
7
, we prove that
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
becomes symmetric and pivotal in the classical limit. In fact, every object becomes "unframed".

The author (HC) is affiliated with and supported by Beijing Institute of Mathematical Sciences and Applications (BIMSA). There are no conflicts of interest to declare at this time.

Data availability statement

There are no data associated with this work.

2
Preliminaries on Hopf monoidal categories

We now begin with a brief introduction on the algebraic structures in this work. Let
(\tilde{C},\otimes,\tilde{I})
denote an (additively complete
\mathbb{C}
-linear) monoidal category with an unit object
\tilde{I}
(see
[
Eti+16
]
). As is conventional, we denote by
\boxtimes
the symmetric monoidal Delign tensor product on linear categories.

We say
\tilde{C}
is a
(strict) bimonoidal category
(cf.
[
CF94
,
DS97
,
BCV16
]
) iff it is equipped with

1.

a strictly coassociative coproduct functor
\tilde{\Delta}:\tilde{C}\rightarrow\tilde{C}\boxtimes\tilde{C}
, and

2.

a counit functor
\epsilon:\tilde{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vect}
,

such that there are invertible natural transformations

\displaystyle\tilde{\Delta}\circ(-\otimes-)\cong(-\otimes-\boxtimes-\otimes-)\circ(1_{\tilde{C}}\otimes\sigma\otimes 1_{\tilde{C}})\circ(\tilde{\Delta}\boxtimes\tilde{\Delta})

(2.1)

\displaystyle(\tilde{\epsilon}\boxtimes 1_{\tilde{C}})\circ\tilde{\Delta}\cong 1_{\tilde{C}},\qquad(1_{\tilde{C}}\boxtimes\tilde{\epsilon})\circ\tilde{\Delta}\cong 1_{\tilde{C}}

which satisfy the obvious coherence conditions against the coassociator,

(\tilde{\Delta}\boxtimes 1_{\tilde{C}})\circ\tilde{\Delta}\cong(1_{\tilde{C}}\boxtimes\tilde{\Delta})\circ\tilde{\Delta},

where
\sigma
swaps the Deligne tensor factors.

Given a bimonoidal category
(\tilde{C},\otimes,\tilde{I},\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\epsilon})
, we now introduce an
antipode functor

\tilde{S}:\tilde{C}\rightarrow\tilde{C}^{\text{m-op,c-op}}
into the monoidal/comonoidal opposite, for which there are invertible natural transformations

(-\otimes-)\circ(\tilde{S}\boxtimes 1_{\tilde{C}})\circ\tilde{\Delta}\cong\tilde{I}\otimes\tilde{\epsilon}\cong(-\otimes-)\circ(1_{\tilde{C}}\boxtimes\tilde{S})\circ\tilde{\Delta},

(2.2)

together with the appropriate coherence relations (cf. a "Hopf algebroid" of
[
DS97
]
). We are now in position to introduce the central algebraic gadget in this paper.

Definition 2.1
.

We say
(\tilde{C},\otimes,\tilde{I},\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{\epsilon},\tilde{S};\tilde{R})
is a
cobraided Hopf category
iff
\tilde{C}
is equipped with a comonoidal natural transformation
\tilde{R}:\tilde{\Delta}\Rightarrow\tilde{\Delta}^{\text{op}}
. We say
\tilde{C}
is
strictly cobraided
iff
\tilde{R}
is invertible, and we say
\tilde{C}
is a
cobraided Hopf dagger category
iff it is equipped with an involutive dagger structure
-^{\dagger}:\tilde{C}\rightarrow\tilde{C}^{\text{op}}
for which the functors
\otimes,\tilde{\Delta},\tilde{S}
are dagger, and their accompanying invertible coherence 2-morphisms are unitary.

In the rest of this paper, we will also often denote
-^{\dagger}=\tilde{S}_{v}
by the dagger structure.

Remark 2.1
.

Similarly, a
Hopf cocategory

C
is a linear additive
co
monoidal
co
category equipped with a compatible monoidal structure
-\otimes-
— and thus making it also into a linear additive bimonoidal category — as well as the appropriate antipodes. A closely related notion of a
Hopf (op)algebroid
was studied in
[
DS97
]
.

\Diamond

The goal of this work is to focus on the structure of the
2-representations
of a strictly cobraided Hopf dagger category
(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
— or just briefly a Hopf category in the following — the structures of which were shown in
[
Che25
]
to naturally appear in the 4-dimensional
2-Chern-Simons theory
.

Cobraiding from 2-
R
-matrices.

Let us unpack the meaning of the
cobraiding

\tilde{R}:\tilde{\Delta}\Rightarrow\tilde{\Delta}^{\text{op}}
, with a specific instance of its construction. Consider the following ingredients:

1.

a
2-
R
-matrix

\tilde{R}\in\tilde{C}\boxtimes\tilde{C}
, which satisfies the
2-Yang-Baxter relations
(cf.
[
CG23
,
Che25
]
)

(\tilde{\Delta}\boxtimes 1)\tilde{R}=\tilde{R}^{13}\otimes\tilde{R}^{12},\qquad(1\boxtimes\tilde{\Delta})\tilde{R}=\tilde{R}^{13}\otimes\tilde{R}^{23},

(2.3)

2.

an invertible 1-morphism witnessing the intertwining relations

\tilde{R}^{\prime}_{\zeta}:\tilde{\Delta}^{\text{op}}(\zeta)\otimes\tilde{R}\cong\tilde{R}\otimes\tilde{\Delta}(\zeta),

which is natural in
\zeta\in\tilde{C}
.

Then, consider a natural isomorphism
\tilde{R}:\tilde{\Delta}\Rightarrow\tilde{\Delta}^{\text{op}}
whose components are given by

\tilde{R}_{\zeta}=\tilde{R}^{\prime}_{\zeta}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{R}}:\tilde{\Delta}(\zeta)\rightarrow\tilde{\Delta}^{\text{op}}(\zeta),

where
\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{R}}=\tilde{R}\otimes-\otimes\tilde{R}^{-1}
is the conjugation action by the 2-
R
-matrix
\tilde{R}
.
1
1

1

It is worth mentioning here that the notion of a
universal
2-
R
-matrix, for the so-called ”Hopf 2-algebras”
[
Wag21
,
Maj12
,
LU96
]
, was defined in
[
CG23
]
. The conditions (
2.3
), (
2.4
) has all be derived in that context.

It can then be directly deduced that the 2-Yang-Baxter (
2.3
) implies the comonoidality of
\tilde{R}
. Moreover, together with the antipode
\tilde{S}
, there exist natural isomorphisms

(\tilde{S}\boxtimes 1)\tilde{R}\otimes\tilde{R}\cong\tilde{I}\boxtimes\tilde{I},\qquad\tilde{R}\otimes(1\boxtimes\tilde{S})\tilde{R}\cong\tilde{I}\boxtimes\tilde{I}

(2.4)

coming from
\tilde{R}^{\prime}
which witnesses the quasitriangularity condition. This specific construction of a strict cobraiding
\tilde{R}
is in fact what appears in the quantization of 2-Chern-Simons theory; see
[
Che25
]
and
Remark
2.3
.

In the next subsection, we will give a brief review of how the structure of a strictly cobraided Hopf dagger category appears in the context of a 4-dimensional topological gauge-field theory. The uninterested readers can skip directly to §
3
.

2.1
Categorical gauge transformations as a Hopf category

This general sentiment — namely that higher-categorical structures should appear in higher-dimensional physics — were well-understood
[
BD95
]
and has seen many successful applications in the past, we here focus on the explicit higher categorical algebra which appears in the
2-Chern-Simons theory
.

By "2-Chern-Simons theory", we refer to the topological higher-gauge theory
[
Jur+19
,
SZ14
,
CL24
]
based on a structure (strict) Lie 2-group
\mathbb{G}=\mathsf{H}\xrightarrow{t}G

[
BL04
,
Por08
]
. On a 4-manifold
M^{4}
, the classical action

S_{2CS}[A,B]=\int_{M^{4}}\langle B,F_{A}-\frac{1}{2}\mu_{1}B\rangle,\qquad\begin{cases}A\in\Omega^{1}(M^{4},\mathfrak{g})\\
B\in\Omega^{2}(M^{4},\mathfrak{h})\end{cases}

involves a tuple of gauge fields
(A,B)
valued in the Lie 2-algebra associated
[
CSX13
]
to
\mathbb{G}
, which constitute (on-shell flat)
\mathbb{G}
2-connection
on
M^{4}

[
SW13
,
Bae+12
,
MP07
,
KS20
]
. It is worth mentioning briefly here that 2-Chern-Simons theory, or higher-gauge theory in general, has been known to be deeply relevant to various fields of physics
[
CG22
,
RS16
,
MOV16
,
MV12
,
GLT21
,
Bae96
,
Bae02
,
BCH19
,
CDI19
,
Son+23
,
Son+21
,
Gai+15
,
LW19
,
KTZ20a
,
WC17
,
Del18
,
Bul+17
,
Boc+21
,
KT17
,
Tho10
,
DRH20
,
KS20
,
SSS12
,
Sch13
]
.

Remark 2.2
.

A Lie 2-group
\mathbb{G}
can also be described in terms of a groupoid

\mathsf{H}\rtimes G\rightrightarrows G,\qquad a\xrightarrow{(a,\gamma)}at(\gamma),

which comes equipped invertible horizontal (group) and vertical (groupoid) multiplications
[
CSX13
]

(a,\gamma)\cdot(a^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})=(aa^{\prime},\gamma(a\rhd\gamma^{\prime})),\qquad(a,\gamma)\circ(at(\gamma),\gamma^{\prime})=(a,\gamma\gamma^{\prime}),

with the units given by
(1,{\bf 1}_{1})
and
{\bf 1}_{a}
for all
a\in G.
They are compatible through the so-called
interchange law

[
BC04
]

((a_{1},\gamma_{1})\cdot(a_{2},\gamma_{2}))\circ((a_{3},\gamma_{3})\cdot(a_{4},\gamma_{4}))=((a_{1},\gamma_{1})\cdot(a_{3},\gamma_{3}))\circ((a_{2},\gamma_{2})\cdot(a_{4},\gamma_{4})),

where
(a_{1},\gamma_{1}),\dots,(a_{4},\gamma_{4})\in\mathbb{G}
are appropriately composable 2-group elements. In this paper, we will often refer to the horizontal multiplication
\cdot
the "product", and the vertical multiplication
\circ
the "composition".

\Diamond

Through the theory of principal 2-bundles
[
Woc11
]
, the surface holonomies associated to the principal
\mathbb{G}
2-bundle on
M^{4}
can be constructed as a
2-functor
which assigns an element of
\mathbb{G}
to a surface-boundary pair
(\Sigma,\gamma)
,

\operatorname{2Hol}_{\mathbb{G}}(\Sigma,\gamma)=(W_{\Sigma},V_{\gamma})\in\mathsf{H}\rtimes G,\qquad tW_{\Sigma}=V_{\partial\Sigma}.

For an explicit construction of these 2-holonomies, see eg.
[
Yek15
,
Bae+12
,
CL24
]
.

In
[
Che25
]
(and reviewed in detail in
[
Che25a
]
), the
combinatorial
quantization framework for Hamiltonian 2-Chern-Simons theory was developed, taking inspiration from the works
[
AGS95
,
AGS95a
]
for 3d Chern-Simoms theory. By "combinatorial", we refer to the discretized 2-holonomies
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}}

[
Bul+17
,
BHL21
,
Boc+21
]
, namely assignments
(b_{f},h_{e})\in\mathsf{H}\rtimes G
of 2-group elements to polygonal face-edge pairs
(e,f)\in\Gamma^{2}
, on the 2-skeleton
\Gamma^{2}
of a simplicialization of a Cauchy slice (ie. a time-like 3-dimensional submanifold of
M^{4}
). See also
[
KT17a
]
for finite 2-groups.

These discrete 2-holonomies were found to inherit a natural action by
2-gauge transformations
. Such 2-gauge transformations are parameterized by assignments of
\mathbb{G}
to 1-simplices,

\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}=\Big\{a_{v}\xrightarrow{(a_{v},\gamma_{e})}a_{v^{\prime}}\mid v\xrightarrow{e}v^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{1}\Big\}

with directed edges
e
with source/target vertices
v,v^{\prime}
. This is a monoidal groupoid, whose composition is given by the confluence of the structures on
\Gamma^{1}
and
\mathbb{G}
,

a_{v}\xrightarrow{(a_{v},\gamma_{e})}a_{v^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{(a_{v^{\prime}},\gamma_{e^{\prime}})}a_{v^{\prime\prime}}=a_{v}\xrightarrow{(a_{v},\gamma_{e}\gamma_{e}^{\prime})}a_{v^{\prime\prime}},

and whose monoidal structure is invertible. In the following, we will simplify our notation and neglect the reference to the source vertex decoration
a_{v}
in the 1-morphisms, and denote by

\zeta=a_{v}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{e}}a_{v^{\prime}}

a decorated 1-graph in
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
.

The natural 2-gauge transformation action of
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
on
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}}
is given by the horizontal/group conjugation:

((a_{v},\gamma_{e}),(h_{e},b_{f}))\mapsto(h^{\prime}_{e},b^{\prime}_{f})=\operatorname{hAd}_{(a_{v},\gamma_{e})}^{-1}(h_{e},b_{f}).

More details on this can be found in the companion paper
[
Che25
]
.
2
2

2

This action in fact makes the 2-functor category

\operatorname{Fun}(\Gamma^{2\leq},\mathbb{G})\simeq\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}}//\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}

into an action 2-groupoid.

2.2
Quantization of the categorical gauge symmetry

The above describes the natural action of the decorated 1-graphs
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
by 2-gauge transformations on the discrete 2-holonomies. This action was then extended in particular to
states
on these 2-holonomies, which can be understood as additive categorical functionals on the combinatorial 2-holonomy configurations. Details of this construction can be found in
[
Che25
]
.

The upshot is that such 2-holonomy states were modelled — in the framework of the
meausreable categories
of Crane-Yetter
[
CY05
,
Yet03
,
Bae+12
]
— as a certain additive Hopf
co
category denoted by
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})
, for which the 2-gauge transformation action induces a module structure

\Lambda:\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}}).

This extends to an additive linear structure

\Lambda_{\zeta\oplus\zeta^{\prime}}=\Lambda_{\zeta}\oplus\Lambda_{\zeta^{\prime}}

for which the 2-gauge parameters are the homogeneous elements (in the sense of
[
SV23
]
). We denote by the additive completion of
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
by "
\mathbb{U}\mathfrak{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
". It was then shown in
[
Che25
]
that
\Lambda
determines
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})
as a
monoidal

\mathbb{U}\mathfrak{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
-module.

Now by following
[
AGS95
]
, given the 2-Chern-Simons Hamiltonian obtained from
S_{2CS}
, one can extract a
2-graded 2-
R
-matrix

[
BSZ13
,
CG23
]
which quantizes into an invertible cobraiding on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})
, and makes the monoidal structure on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})
non-symmetric (denoted
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
). For an idea of what this cobraiding is, see
Remark
2.3
). This gives rise to a deformation quantization
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})\rightsquigarrow\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})
induced from the Lie 2-bialgebra
(\mathfrak{G};r)
underlying 2-Chern-Simons action
[
CG22a
]
.

Remark 2.3
.

Let us elaborate a bit more on the cobraiding. In
[
Che25
]
, we have defined a (measureable) Hopf cocategory
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})
whose objects are given by sheaves
\phi=\Gamma_{\mathbb{G}}[[\hbar]]
of smooth
C(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
-module algebras over
\mathbb{G}
, where
C_{q}(\mathbb{G})=C(\mathbb{G})\otimes\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
denotes the quantized function algebra on
\mathbb{G}
(see
[
CG23
]
). The components of the cobraiding
R
at
\phi
is, by definition, a measureable morphism

R_{\phi}:\Delta_{\phi}=\bigoplus\phi_{(1)}\boxtimes\phi_{(2)}\rightarrow\Delta_{\phi}^{\text{op}}=\bigoplus\phi_{(2)}\boxtimes\phi_{(1)}

given by a sheaf of bounded linear operators

(R_{\phi})_{\mathrm{z},\mathrm{z}^{\prime}}:\bigoplus(\phi_{(1)})_{\mathrm{z}}\boxtimes(\phi_{(2)})_{\mathrm{z}^{\prime}}\rightarrow\bigoplus(\phi_{(2)})_{\mathrm{z}}\boxtimes(\phi_{(1)})_{\mathrm{z}^{\prime}}

at each stalk
(\mathrm{z},\mathrm{z}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{G}^{\times 2}
.
3
3

3

Here the structure sheaf
C_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\times 2})\cong C_{q}(\mathbb{G})\bar{\otimes}C_{q}(\mathbb{G})
on
\mathbb{G}^{\times 2}
is defined in terms of the topological tensor product
\bar{\otimes}
, which is given by norm-completing the usual tensor product of the measureable
L^{2}
-sections.
Given the invertibility of
R
, we can decompose the *-automorphism

R_{\phi}=R_{\phi}^{\prime}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{R(\phi)},\qquad R(\phi)\in\bigoplus\phi_{(1)}\boxtimes\phi_{(2)}

into an "inner" and "outer" part. The sheaf
R\in\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})\boxtimes\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})
formed by all of the sections
R(\phi)
then constitute precisely the so-called "
2-
R
-matrix
" mentioned earlier in the beginning of §
2
.

\Diamond

In light of this deformation, for
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})
to remain as a
monoidal
module under 2-gauge transformations
\Lambda
, the category
\mathbb{U}\mathfrak{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
must itself receive a non-trivial quantum deformation. This leads to the introduction of an invertible cobraiding
\tilde{R}:\tilde{\Delta}\Rightarrow\tilde{\Delta}^{\text{op}}
which satisfies the following conditions against
\Lambda
:

1.

the presence of a coherent invertible
module tensorator

\varpi:(-\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)\circ\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}}\cong\Lambda\circ(-\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-):\tilde{\mathbb{U}}_{q}\mathfrak{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})^{\boxtimes 2}\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}}),

and that

2.

the cobraidings satisfy

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}}R\big(\Delta\big)\cong\Lambda_{\tilde{R}\big(\tilde{\Delta}\big)}\Delta:\tilde{\mathbb{U}}_{q}\mathfrak{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{2}})^{\boxtimes 2}.

Together with the
2-dagger structure
on the 2-skeleton
\Gamma^{2}
induced by orientation reversal and framing reversal (see
Example 5.5
of
[
Fer+24
]
), the following was then proven in
[
Che25
]
.

Proposition 2.2
.

The categorical quantum gauge symmetries
\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
of 2-Chern-Simons theory give rise to a strictly cobraided Hopf dagger category, graded by the groupoid
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
(cf.
[
SV23
]
) — where the antipode
\tilde{S}
and the dagger structure
-^{\dagger}=\tilde{S}_{v}
are induced by the 2-dagger structure on the lattice
\Gamma
.

Remark 2.4
.

We note here that the above Hopf categorical structure of
\tilde{C}=\tilde{C}^{\Gamma}
requires one to specify an underlying lattice
\Gamma
, but it does
not
depend on which lattice it is. Particularly, if
\Gamma^{1}=\{v\xrightarrow{e}v\}
consist of a single edge loop based at a vertex
v\in\Gamma^{0}
, the homogeneous elements of the Hopf category
\tilde{C}
corresponds to a single copy of
\mathbb{G}
. This led the author to call
[
Che25
]
, in this case, the
categorical quantum enveloping algebra

\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}
, where
\mathfrak{G}=\operatorname{Lie}\mathbb{G}
denotes the Lie 2-algebra underlying the Lie 2-group
[
CSX13
,
BSZ13
,
CG22a
]
. This is only a suggestive notation for now, but a future work will substantiate this notation by studying its categorical quantum duality with the
categorical quantum coordinate ring
. This latter notion was defined in a much more concrete manner in
[
Che25
]
.

\Diamond

2.3
Hopf structure on the quantum 2-gauge symmetries

In light of
Remark
2.4
, a special case of
Proposition
2.2
is then the following.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
\Gamma^{1}=\big\{v\xrightarrow{e}v\big\}
denote a 1-graph with a single loop edge
e
. The corresponding 2-gauge transformations on
\Gamma^{1}
gives rise to a
\mathbb{G}
-graded strictly-cobraided Hopf dagger category
\Big(\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}=\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G},\cdot,\tilde{I},\Delta,\tilde{\epsilon},\tilde{S};\tilde{R}\Big)
.

We call the Hopf category
\tilde{C}=\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}
the
categorical quantum symmetries,
and serves as the central motivation for
Definition
2.1
.

The classical limit.

Recall
q
denotes the formal deformation parameter which deforms the coprodut functor
\Delta
on
\tilde{C}
. As such, it is worth mentioning that in the classical limit
q\rightarrow 1
we have

1.

\tilde{C}
becomes cocommutative,

2.

\tilde{R}\rightarrow\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{id}
becomes trivial, and

3.

\tilde{S}
becomes unipotent.

These facts will become important later in §
7
; they are analogues of the properties of ordinary quantum groups
[
Wor88
,
Maj11
]
.

We emphasize here that much of what follows should hold with
\tilde{C}
replaced by a generic (ie. weak) Hopf dagger category equipped with a weak/lax cobraiding. However, we will prove several characterization results for the 2-representations specifically for the case where
\tilde{C}
describes quantum 2-gauge transformations.

3
Unitary 2-representations of
\tilde{C}

We say a linear finite semisimple category
{\cal D}
(ie. a Kapranov-Voevodsky 2-vector space
[
KV94
]
) is a finite-dimensional
2-representation
of
\tilde{C}
iff it is equipped with a lax monoidal functor
\rho:\tilde{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{End}({\cal D})
, or equivalently a
\tilde{C}
-module structure
\rhd:\tilde{C}\times{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}
such that

\rho(\zeta)(d)=\zeta\rhd d,\qquad\forall\penalty\ \zeta\in\tilde{C},\penalty\ d\in{\cal D}.

We will often use both descriptions interchangeably. Note we do not a priori require
{\cal D}
to be representable as sheaves over some
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
-space
P

[
NW11
,
Sch11
]
; this notion will become important elsewhere, but not here.

These 2-representations form a 2-category denoted by
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C})
, in which the 1-morphisms are module functors
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}
equipped with intertwining natural transformations
F_{\zeta}:F(\zeta\rhd-)\rightarrow\zeta\rhd F(-)
for each
\zeta\in\tilde{C}
, and the 2-morphisms are module natural transformations
\alpha:F\Rightarrow F^{\prime}
which commute with
F_{\zeta},F^{\prime}_{\zeta}
. Such 2-representation 2-categories and their applications have been studied extensively for
finite
2-groups
\mathbb{G}
in, eg.,
[
Bar+24
,
BBG23
,
HXZ24
,
DT24
,
Che23
]
, for which
\operatorname{2Rep}(\mathbb{G})
is known to be finite semisimple (in fact fusion; see
[
DR18
]
). In contrast, however, we emphasize here that it is so far unknown whether
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
itself is finite semisimple as a 2-category — it just
contains
finite semisimple objects.

Throughout the following, we will use the "geometric/left-to-right convention for products
\boxtimes
of objects and the "functorial"/right-to-left convention for composition
\circ,\bullet
of 1-, 2-morphisms (see
[
DR18
]
for a discussion on the distinction). In accordance with
Remark
2.4
, we will without loss of generality consider
\Gamma^{1}=\{v\xrightarrow{e}v\}
consisting of a single loop, and
\tilde{C}=\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}
. The main results in
[
Neu97
,
CG23
]
then give us the following.

Theorem 3.1
.

The 2-category
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
of 2-representations of the cobraided Hopf category
\tilde{C}=\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}
, equipped with a cobraiding natural transformation
\tilde{R}:\tilde{\Delta}\Rightarrow\sigma\tilde{\Delta}
, is braided monoidal.

We will give a brief review in §
3.1
of how the coproduct/cobraiding on
\tilde{C}
introduce respectively the monoidal/braiding structures on
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
.

However, here we can do better, because we have access to the antipode
\tilde{S}:\tilde{C}\rightarrow\tilde{C}^{\text{m-op,c-op}}
and the dagger structure on
\tilde{C}
. The goal in this paper is to show that, over the
\mathbb{C}
-linear category
\mathsf{Hilb}
of Hilbert spaces (namely we work with
\mathsf{2Hilb}

[
Bae96a
,
GK06
]
instead of
\mathsf{2Vect}
), these give rise to the notions of compatible
duals and adjoints
in
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
(cf.
[
DR18
]
).

3.1
Some monoidal and braided preliminaries

Let us first describe briefly the monoidal and braided structures of
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
as following from the Hopf structure of
\tilde{C}
, as we shall use them explicitly later. Details of these descriptions can be found in
[
Neu97
,
CG23
]
. We shall work in the linear context, in which all 2-representations
{\cal D}\in\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C})
are
\mathsf{Hilb}
-modules.

The key observation throughout this section is the fact that the action functor
\rhd
sends objects in
\tilde{C}
to endofunctors and morphisms to endonatural transformations. Thus we can record natural transformations by the edge parameters
{\cal E}
by the following

\zeta\rhd({\cal D}\xrightarrow{F}{\cal D}^{\prime})=(a_{v}\rhd{\cal D})\xrightarrow{\gamma_{e}\rhd F}(a_{v}^{\prime}\rhd{\cal D}^{\prime})=\hbox to182.91pt{\vbox to44.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 91.45549pt\lower-23.14995pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

for each functor
F\in\operatorname{Hom}({\cal D},{\cal D}^{\prime})
, where
\zeta=a_{v}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{e}}a_{v^{\prime}}\in\tilde{C}
is written in terms of its source and target.

In the following, we keep track of the
\tilde{C}
-module coherence conditions satisfied by the natural transformation
F_{a_{v}}:F(a_{v}\rhd-)\Rightarrow a_{v}\rhd^{\prime}F(-)

[
DT24
]
by the following commuting diagram

4

This notation is suggestive. Indeed, for an endofunctor
F:{\cal I}\rightarrow{\cal I}
on the tensor unit
{\cal I}
(defined later),
F_{a_{v}}:a_{v}\rhd-\circ F\circ a_{v}^{-1}\rhd-\cong F
defines an endo-natural transformation on
F
and hence determines an action of
{\cal V}
on
\operatorname{End}({\cal I})
. See also
[
Che23
,
BBG23
]
.

(3.1)

We call this the
{\cal E}
-structures
of the
\tilde{C}
-module functors; here "
{\cal E}
" refers to the
e
dge decorations/1-morphisms in
\tilde{C}=\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}
.

3.1.1
Tensor products

Recall from §
2.3
the coproduct functor
\tilde{\Delta}
on
\tilde{C}
. Putting

\tilde{\Delta}_{0}=(\iota\otimes\iota)\tilde{\Delta}\mid_{{\cal V}},\qquad\tilde{\Delta}_{1}^{l}=(\iota\otimes\pi)\tilde{\Delta}\mid_{{\cal E}},\qquad\tilde{\Delta}^{r}_{1}=(\pi\otimes\iota)\tilde{\Delta}\mid_{\cal E},

we write for any
\tilde{C}
-module category
{\cal D}
and functor
F:{\cal A}\rightarrow{\cal A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}\rhd({\cal A}\boxtimes{\cal D}\xrightarrow{F\boxtimes{\cal D}}{\cal A}^{\prime}\boxtimes{\cal D})=(\tilde{\Delta}_{0})_{a_{v}}\rhd({{\cal A}\boxtimes{\cal D}})\xrightarrow{(\tilde{\Delta}_{1}^{l})_{\gamma_{e}}\rhd(F\boxtimes{\cal D})}(\tilde{\Delta}_{0})_{a_{v}^{\prime}}\rhd({{\cal A}^{\prime}\boxtimes{\cal D}}),

\displaystyle\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}\rhd({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}\xrightarrow{{\cal D}\boxtimes F}{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{\prime})=(\tilde{\Delta}_{0})_{a_{v}}\rhd({{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}})\xrightarrow{(\tilde{\Delta}_{1}^{r})_{\gamma_{e}}\rhd({\cal D}\boxtimes F)}(\tilde{\Delta}_{0})_{a_{v}^{\prime}}\rhd({{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{\prime}})

where
\zeta=a_{v}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{e}}a_{v}^{\prime}\in\tilde{C}
.

The naturality of this definition, as well as the compatibility against the
\tilde{C}
-module associator

\zeta\rhd(\zeta^{\prime}\rhd-)\xrightarrow{\sim}(\zeta\cdot\zeta^{\prime})\rhd-,

follow respectively from the fact that
\tilde{\Delta}
defines a functor and the bimonoidal axioms for
\tilde{C}
. These facts were proven in Lemmas 6.11, 6.12 in
[
CG23
]
; see also
[
Neu97
]
. There is then also a natural
interchanger
of functors
F:{\cal A}\rightarrow{\cal A}^{\prime}
and
G:{\cal B}\rightarrow{\cal B}^{\prime}
,

\upsilon_{G,F}:({\cal A}^{\prime}\boxtimes G)\circ(F\boxtimes{\cal B})\Rightarrow(F\boxtimes{\cal B}^{\prime})\circ({\cal A}\boxtimes G),

which can always be chosen to be invertible/equivalences.

Remark 3.1
.

Take any pair of functors
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}
and
G:{\cal A}\rightarrow{\cal A}^{\prime}
. The invertibility of the interchanger
\upsilon_{F,G}
implies that the tensor product
F\boxtimes G:{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{\prime}
is well-defined

F\boxtimes G=({{\cal D}^{\prime}}\boxtimes G)\circ(F\boxtimes{\cal A})\cong(F\boxtimes{{\cal A}^{\prime}})\circ({{\cal D}}\boxtimes G)

up to 2-isomorphism; this is called
nudging
of functors in
[
DR18
]
. In the following, we will assume that all functors between monoidal products of
\tilde{C}
-module categories can be written in this way, ie. using nudging. This is a 2-categorical version of the condition Definition 1.7 (b) in
[
Del07
]
.

\tilde{C}

\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
by
\alpha^{\tilde{C}}
. The strict coassociativity of
\tilde{\Delta}
gives rise to an invertible natural transformations fitting into commutative squares of the form

(3.2)

see Lemma 6.15 of
[
CG23
]
, and also
[
Neu97
,
Gur06
]
. Since
\tilde{C}
is strict, these associators and pentagonators
[
DT24
,
KTZ20
]
are always invertible and have identity components. We shall therefore suppress them in the following.

Remark 3.2
.

When
\mathbb{G}
is a weakly-associative smooth 2-group
[
Sch11
]
, its associator
\tau:G^{\times 3}\rightarrow\mathsf{H}
(ie. the representative of its
Postnikov class
) directly contributes to a
non-invertible

\tilde{C}
-module associator
\alpha^{\tilde{C}}
through the vertical maps in (
3.2
). The monoidal witness
\zeta\rhd(\zeta^{\prime}\rhd-)\Rightarrow(\zeta\cdot\zeta^{\prime})\rhd-
must satisfy a module pentagon equation against this associator. These non-invertible 1-morphisms must therefore be kept track of when
\mathbb{G}
is weakly-associative.

\Diamond

The counit functor
\tilde{\epsilon}:\tilde{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{Hilb}
identifies a distinguished object
{\cal I}\in\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C})
as the trivial 2-representation
a_{v}\rhd{\cal I}=\epsilon(a_{v})\otimes{\cal I}\cong{\cal I}
in terms of the
\mathsf{Hilb}
-module structure of
{\cal I}
. Furthermore,
\epsilon
also selects a counit
({\bf 1}_{a_{v}})_{e}
over each object
a_{v}\in\tilde{C}
(ie. the identity arrow), such that the identity endofunctor
1_{\cal D}\in\operatorname{End}({\cal D})
transforms as

\zeta\rhd({\cal D}\xrightarrow{1_{\cal D}}{\cal D})=(a_{v}\rhd{\cal D})\xrightarrow{\epsilon_{a_{v}}\cdot 1_{\cal D}}(a_{v}\rhd{\cal D})=(a_{v}\rhd{\cal D})\xrightarrow{1_{\cal D}}(a_{v}\rhd{\cal D}).

Concretely,
\epsilon_{\gamma_{e}}
is represented as an invertible linear map, and it "acts" on
1_{\cal D}
as an element of
\mathsf{Hilb}
. The counitality axiom
(\tilde{\epsilon}\otimes 1)\circ\tilde{\Delta}=(1\otimes\tilde{\epsilon})\circ\tilde{\Delta}=\operatorname{id}
gives rise to the following invertible
\tilde{C}
-module unitors

such that the usual triangle axioms follow from the counit axioms and coassociativity,

(\tilde{\epsilon}\otimes 1\otimes 1)(\tilde{\Delta}\otimes 1)\tilde{\Delta}=\tilde{\Delta}=(\tilde{\epsilon}\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes\tilde{\Delta})\tilde{\Delta},\qquad\text{etc.}

3.1.2
Braiding

We now briefly introduce the braiding structure. Recall in §
2
that we are presently working under the assumption that the cobriading
\tilde{R}
is constructed from the quantum 2-
R
-matrix on
\tilde{C}
, which we as an abuse of notation also denote by
\tilde{R}\in\tilde{C}\boxtimes\tilde{C}
. The braiding map then takes the form

c=\text{flip}\circ(\rho\boxtimes\rho^{\prime})\tilde{R},\qquad\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C},\tilde{R}),

where the flip map
{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{\prime}\mapsto{\cal D}^{\prime}\boxtimes{\cal D}
swaps the Delign tensor product factors.
More explicitly, writing

\tilde{R}_{0}=\tilde{R}\mid_{{\cal V}\otimes{\cal V}},\qquad\tilde{R}_{1}^{r}=\tilde{R}\mid_{{\cal E}\otimes{\cal V}},\qquad\tilde{R}_{1}^{l}=\tilde{R}\mid_{{\cal V}\otimes{\cal E}},

we put

\displaystyle c_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}=\text{flip}(\tilde{R}_{0}(\rhd-\boxtimes\rhd-)),

\displaystyle c_{F,{\cal D}}=\text{flip}(\tilde{R}_{1}^{l}(\rhd-\boxtimes\rhd-)),\qquad c_{{\cal D},F}=\text{flip}(\tilde{R}_{1}^{r}(\rhd-\boxtimes\rhd-)),

where we recall the vertex transforms act by natural transformations on functors. Hence given functors
F\in\operatorname{Hom}({\cal D},{\cal D}^{\prime})
and
F^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Hom}({\cal A},{\cal A}^{\prime})
, we can then write

\displaystyle c({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}\xrightarrow{F\boxtimes{\cal A}}{\cal D}^{\prime}\boxtimes{\cal A})=c_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A})\xrightarrow{c_{F,{\cal A}}(F\boxtimes{\cal A})}c_{{\cal D}^{\prime},{\cal A}}({\cal D}^{\prime}\boxtimes{\cal A}),

\displaystyle c({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}\xrightarrow{{\cal D}\boxtimes F^{\prime}}{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{\prime})=c_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A})\xrightarrow{c_{{\cal D},F^{\prime}}({\cal D}\boxtimes F^{\prime})}c_{{\cal D},{\cal A}^{\prime}}({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{\prime}).

The fact that these define
\tilde{C}
-module functors/natural transformations is a result of the quasitriangularity condition (
2.3
). The naturality of
\tilde{R}
as a cobraiding transformation on
\tilde{C}
implies that these braiding structures fit into the following squares,

where
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime}:{\cal A}\rightarrow{\cal A}^{\prime}
. See Lemmas 7.4, 7.3 in
[
CG23
]
or
[
Neu97
]
; the graphical representation of these 2-morphisms can be found in fig. 55 (d) in
[
BMS24
]
.

Due to the strictness of the cobraiding, the leftover part
\tilde{R}^{\prime}
of the cobraiding can be seen to contribute directly to the invertible hexagonator
\Omega
, which witnesses the hexagon relation/third Reidemeister move
[
BL03
,
KTZ20
,
CG23
,
Che23
]
. The naturality of the braiding gives a
braid-exchange
2-morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\cal D}_{1}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{2})\boxtimes{\cal D}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}_{3}\boxtimes({\cal D}_{1}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{({\cal D}_{2}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{1})\boxtimes{\cal D}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}_{3}\boxtimes({\cal D}_{2}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{{\cal D}_{1}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{2},{\cal D}_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{{\cal D}_{1},{\cal D}_{2}}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cal D}_{3}\boxtimes c_{{\cal D}_{1},{\cal D}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{{\cal D}_{2}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{1},{\cal D}_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{c_{{\cal D}_{1},{\cal D}_{2}},{\cal D}_{3}}}

(3.3)

for each object
{\cal D}_{1},{\cal D}_{2},{\cal D}_{3}\in\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{D})
, which relates the two hexagonators through the following invertible 2-morphism (see
[
KTZ20
,
CG23
]
, also fig. 55 (c) in
[
BMS24
]
)

\Omega_{c_{{\cal D}_{1},{\cal D}_{2}}|{\cal D}_{3}}=\Omega_{{\cal D}_{1}|{\cal D}_{3}{\cal D}_{2}}^{-1}\bullet c_{c_{{\cal D}_{1},{\cal D}_{2}},{\cal D}_{3}}\bullet\Omega_{{\cal D}_{1}|{\cal D}_{2}{\cal D}_{3}}.

Moreover, (
2.3
) and the strict coassociativity of
\tilde{C}
also allows us to deduce the compatibility of the hexagonators against tensor products,

\displaystyle\Omega_{({\cal D}_{3}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{4})|{\cal D}_{1}{\cal D}_{2}}\bullet({\cal D}_{3}\boxtimes\Omega_{{\cal D}_{4}|{\cal D}_{1}{\cal D}_{2}}\circ\Omega_{{\cal D}_{3}|{\cal D}_{1}{\cal D}_{2}}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{4}):

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad(c_{{\cal D}_{2},{\cal D}_{4}}\circ c_{{\cal D}_{1},{\cal D}_{4}})\circ(c_{{\cal D}_{1},{\cal D}_{3}}\circ c_{{\cal D}_{2},{\cal D}_{3}})\Rightarrow c_{{\cal D}_{1}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{2},{\cal D}_{3}\boxtimes{\cal D}_{4}},

(3.4)

for any quadruple of objects
{\cal D}_{1},\dots,{\cal D}_{4}
. In the context of a Hopf 2-algebra, the four braided monoidal coherence axioms
[
Gur11
,
KTZ20
]
were explicitly checked to hold in Theorem 7.11 of
[
CG23
]
, hence we will not reproduce them here.

The following is true in any braided monoidal 2-category
[
Bat08
]
.

Proposition 3.2
.

The endomorphism category
\operatorname{End}({\cal I})
of the unit 2-representation
{\cal I}\in\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
is symmetric.

3.2
Adjunction of 2-representations

Recall the notion of 2-Hilbert spaces
\mathsf{2Hilb}
in
[
Bae96a
]
; see also
[
Che+24
]
for a more recent and accurate account. Let us start light by studying the adjoints. We shall inherit the left-/right-adjoints for the hom-categories in
\mathsf{2Hilb}
from the left-/right-dualities in
\mathsf{Hilb}
--- ie. that of taking the dual or the predual Hilbert spaces.
5
5

5

For
V\in\mathsf{Hilb}
, the left- and right-duals coincide and we in fact have non-canonical isomorphisms
V\cong V^{*}\cong\,^{*}V
due to Riesz representation theorem. Hence we technically do not need to distinguish between left- and right-adjoints here, but we do it anyway for bookkeeping.

The reason for this is the following: since
\mathsf{Hilb}
itself is bi-involutive
[
JP17
]
, making
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
2-
\mathsf{Hilb}
-enriched will automatically make it into a
dagger 2-category

[
SS23
,
Fer+24
]
. In fact, this was the original motivation for higher-dagger structures.

Leveraging this observation, we shall describe adjunctions on the
\tilde{C}
-module functors, such that they come equipped with the proper
{\cal E}
-structures, in the following. This is accomplished by
orientation reversal
.

Orientation reversal.

Observe that, under an orientation reversal
e\mapsto\bar{e}
, the appropriate swaps are achieved

(\bar{\zeta})_{(v,e)}=a_{v}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{\bar{e}}}a_{v}

for each
\zeta=a_{v}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{e}}a_{v}^{\prime}\in\tilde{C}
. The induced involution
\tilde{C}\rightarrow\tilde{C}^{\text{op}}
is precisely the dagger structure
\tilde{S}_{v}=-^{\dagger}
.

Now suppose, for each
{\cal D}
, its
\tilde{C}
-action functor
\rho=\rhd
satisfy the following
unitarity property

(a_{v}\rhd-)^{\dagger}=\,^{\dagger}(a_{v}\rhd-)=a_{v}\rhd-,\qquad\forall\penalty\ a_{v}\in{\cal V},

(3.5)

then we define the following
{\cal E}
-structures of the adjunctions

\displaystyle\zeta\rhd({\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{F^{\dagger}}{\cal D})=(a_{v}^{\prime}\rhd)^{\dagger}{\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(\gamma_{\bar{e}}\rhd)^{\dagger}(F^{\dagger})}(a_{v}\rhd)^{\dagger}{\cal D},

\displaystyle\zeta\rhd({\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\,{}^{\dagger}F}{\cal D})=\,^{\dagger}(a_{v}^{\prime}\rhd){\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\,{}^{\dagger}(\gamma_{\bar{e}}\rhd)(\,^{\dagger}F)}\,^{\dagger}(a_{v}\rhd){\cal D}.

The following left-/right-adjunction-mates of
\tilde{C}
-module natural transformations
6
6

6

This means that
\alpha:F\Rightarrow G
intertwines
\rho(\gamma_{e})
, and commutes with the natural transformations
F_{a_{v}},G_{a_{v}}
.

\alpha:F\Rightarrow G
,

(F\xRightarrow{\alpha}G)^{\dagger}=(G^{\dagger}\xRightarrow{\alpha^{\dagger}}F^{\dagger}),\qquad\,^{\dagger}(F\xRightarrow{\alpha}G)=(\,^{\dagger}G\xRightarrow{\,{}^{\dagger}\alpha}\,^{\dagger}F),

(3.6)

are themselves
\tilde{C}
-module natural transformations. This follows directly from the naturality of the duals in
\mathsf{Hilb}
. We shall mainly focus on the left-adjoint
-^{\dagger}
in the following.

From the geometry (or the definition of the dagger
-^{\dagger}=\tilde{S}_{v}
), orientation reversal swaps the sources and targets of the arrows in
\tilde{C}
. The composition of arrows in
\tilde{C}
then then leads to the condition

(F\circ G)^{\dagger}=G^{\dagger}\circ F^{\dagger}

(3.7)

satisfied by the adjunctions. Furthermore, given the unit representation
\tilde{\epsilon}:\tilde{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{Hilb}
lands in
real
Hilbert spaces (ie. those which are self-dual under
-^{\dagger}
), this implies

1_{\cal I}^{\dagger}=1_{{\cal I}}.

This can be understood as a certain
reality condition
on the unit
{\cal I}
.

To be clear, the above is describing the proper
{\cal E}
-structure – as given by the orientation reversal/dagger involution structure — for the adjoints of the
\tilde{C}
-module functors, such that the adjunction co/unit 2-morphisms (called "folds for the adjunctions in the following) have a canonical
\tilde{C}
-module structure.

3.2.1
Folds for the adjoints

Now take a 1-graph
v\xrightarrow{e}v^{\prime}
and its orientation reversal. Their composition bounds a contractible 2-cell which is null-homotopic,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{v^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

On the other hand, the decorations on the 1-graphs by construction respect their groupoid compositions, the
{\cal E}
-structure of
F^{\dagger}\circ F
is given by
((\gamma_{\bar{e}}\rhd-)^{\dagger}\bullet(\gamma_{e^{\prime}}\rhd-))(F)
. We are therefore led to the following notion.

Definition 3.3
.

A
\tilde{C}
-module functor
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}
is said to have
planar-unitary structure
iff

1.

{\cal D},{\cal D}^{\prime}
have equipped
{\cal V}
-action functors
\rho=\rhd,\rho^{\prime}=\rhd^{\prime}
that satisfy (
3.5
), and

2.

F
come equipped with the following 2-morphisms

e_{F}:F^{\dagger}\circ F\Rightarrow 1_{\cal D},\qquad\iota_{F}:1_{{\cal D}^{\prime}}\Rightarrow F\circ\,^{\dagger}F,

called
adjunction-folds
, such that they induce the following commutative diagrams,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho\textquoteright(a_{v})^{-1}\circ(F^{\dagger}\circ F)\circ\rho(a_{v})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho\textquoteright(a_{v})^{-1}\circ 1_{\cal D}\circ\rho(a_{v})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F^{\dagger}\circ F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\cal D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho\textquoteright(a_{v}^{\prime})^{-1}\circ(F^{\dagger}\circ F)\circ\rho(a_{v}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho\textquoteright(a_{v}^{\prime})^{-1}\circ 1_{\cal D}\circ\rho(a_{v}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\textquoteright(\gamma_{e})^{-1}\circ e_{F}\circ\rho(\gamma_{e})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F^{\dagger}\circ F)_{a_{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{e}^{\dagger}\bullet\gamma_{e})F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{\cal D})_{a_{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{e}^{\dagger}\bullet\gamma_{e})1_{\cal D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F^{\dagger}\circ F)_{a_{v}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\textquoteright(\gamma_{e\textquoteright})^{-1}\circ e_{F}\circ\rho(\gamma_{e\textquoteright})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{\cal D})_{a_{v}^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho\textquoteright(a_{v})^{-1}\circ(F\circ\,^{\dagger}F)\circ\rho(a_{v})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho\textquoteright(a_{v})^{-1}\circ 1_{\cal D}\circ\rho(a_{v})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F\circ\,^{\dagger}F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1_{\cal D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho\textquoteright(a_{v}^{\prime})^{-1}\circ(F\circ\,^{\dagger}F)\circ\rho(a_{v}^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho\textquoteright(a_{v}^{\prime})^{-1}\circ 1_{\cal D}\circ\rho(a_{v}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\circ\,^{\dagger}F)_{a_{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{e}\bullet\,^{\dagger}\gamma_{e})F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\textquoteright(\gamma_{e})^{-1}\circ\iota_{F}\circ\rho(\gamma_{e})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{\cal D})_{a_{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{e}\bullet\,^{\dagger}\gamma_{e})1_{\cal D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\circ\,^{\dagger}F)_{a_{v}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1_{\cal D})_{a_{v}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho\textquoteright(\gamma_{e\textquoteright})^{-1}\circ\iota_{F}\circ\rho(\gamma_{e\textquoteright})}

Here we have used a shorthand
(\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{\dagger}\bullet\gamma_{e})F=(\rho^{\dagger}(\gamma_{\bar{e}})^{-1}\circ F^{\dagger}\circ\rho^{\dagger}(\gamma_{\bar{e}}))\bullet(\rho(\gamma_{e})^{-1}\circ F\circ\rho(\gamma_{e}))
, and similarly for
(\gamma_{e}\bullet\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{\dagger})
.

3.

A 2-morphism
\alpha:F\Rightarrow G
between functors
F,G:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}
is called
planar-unitary
(or just unitary) if it has left-/right-inverses given by the left-/right-adjunction-mates

\alpha^{\dagger}\bullet\alpha=\operatorname{id}_{F},\qquad\alpha\bullet\,^{\dagger}\alpha=\operatorname{id}_{G}.

Note this follows directly from the naturalty of the adjunction-folds
e,\iota
if
F,G
are themselves planar-unitary.

The 2-category
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
is said to have
planar-unitarity
if all of its functors are planar-unitary.

By the invertibility of 2-gauge transformations,
\rho(\gamma_{e})^{-1}\rho(\gamma_{e})={\bf 1}_{e}
is equivalent to the trivial edge transform for all
\tilde{C}
-actions functors
\rho
. The above definition then implies that the adjunction-folds are intertwining

e_{F}\bullet(\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{\dagger}\bullet\gamma_{e})=({\bf 1}_{e}\rhd-)\bullet e_{F},\qquad\iota_{F}\bullet({\bf 1}_{e}\rhd-)=(\gamma_{e}\bullet\gamma_{\bar{e}}^{\dagger})\bullet\iota_{F}.

(3.8)

Since the identity functor itself
1_{\cal D}
satisfies
1_{\cal D}\circ 1_{\cal D}=1_{\cal D}
and is attached the trivial
{\cal E}
-structure
a_{v}\rhd-=\operatorname{id}
, we also have

e_{1_{\cal D}}=\operatorname{id}_{1_{\cal D}},\qquad\iota_{1_{\cal D}}=\operatorname{id}_{1_{\cal D}}.

In conjunction with (
3.7
), the following null-homotopies

lead to the following compatibility

e_{F\circ G}=e_{G}\bullet(G^{\dagger}\circ e_{F}\circ G),\qquad\iota_{F\circ G}=(F\circ\iota_{G}\circ F^{\dagger})\bullet\iota_{F}

(3.9)

for composable planar-unitary
\tilde{C}
-module functors
{\cal D}\xrightarrow{G}{\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{F}{\cal D}^{\prime\prime}
. In the following, all 2-representations of
\tilde{C}
will be planar-unitary.

Similar statements as above of course hold for the right-adjoints of
\tilde{C}
-module functors. In particular, we also have the adjunction-folds

\overline{e}_{G}:G\circ\,^{\dagger}G\Rightarrow 1_{{\cal D}^{\prime}},\qquad\overline{\iota}_{F}:\,^{\dagger}F\circ F\Rightarrow 1_{\cal D}.

However, since we know that adjunctions are left-/right-involutive
\,{}^{\dagger}F^{\dagger}\cong F
, we see that

\overline{e}_{G}=e_{\,{}^{\dagger}G},\qquad\overline{\iota}_{F}=\iota_{\,{}^{\dagger}F};

this is part of the conditions for "planar-pivotality" for a 2-category in
[
DR18
]
.

3.2.2
Snake equations for the adjunctions

We now turn to the left- and right-adjoint-mate 2-morphisms.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\alpha:F\Rightarrow G
be a 2-morphism in
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C},\tilde{R})
. Recall the left-/right-adjoint-mates in (
3.6
); we have

\displaystyle\alpha^{\dagger}=(e_{G}\circ F^{\dagger})\bullet(G^{\dagger}\circ\alpha\circ F^{\dagger})\bullet(G^{\dagger}\circ\iota_{F})

\,{}^{\dagger}\alpha=(\,^{\dagger}F\circ{e}_{\,{}^{\dagger}G})\bullet(\,^{\dagger}F\circ\alpha\circ\,^{\dagger}G)\bullet({\iota}_{\,{}^{\dagger}F}\circ\,^{\dagger}G).

Moreover, they coincide.

Proof.

By planar-unitarity, we only need to show that the right-adjunction-mate
\,{}^{\dagger}\alpha
coincides with

\,{}^{\dagger}\alpha^{\prime}=(\,^{\dagger}F\circ\overline{e}_{G})\bullet(\,^{\dagger}F\circ\alpha\circ\,^{\dagger}G)\bullet(\overline{\iota}_{F}\circ\,^{\dagger}G).

Further, since both sides of the above equations are by construction the same natural transformations between the adjoints of
F,G
, we only need to show that they both also have the same
{\cal E}
-structures (
3.1
).

The left-hand sides
\alpha^{\dagger},\,^{\dagger}\alpha^{\prime}
by definition (
3.6
) intertwines between the
{\cal E}
-structures
a_{v}^{\dagger}=(a_{v}\rhd-)^{\dagger}
on
F,G
. By chasing through some diagrams in the definition of planar-unitarity, (
3.8
) states that the right-hand sides have the same
{\cal E}
-structure, hence we achieve the desired equality. These adjunction-mates coincide because
\tilde{S}_{v}
is unipotent, which implies
-^{\dagger}
is involutive.
∎

By computing the double-adjoint
\alpha^{\dagger\dagger}
in two different ways,

\big((e_{G}\circ F^{\dagger})\bullet(G^{\dagger}\circ\alpha\circ F^{\dagger})\bullet(G^{\dagger}\circ\iota_{F})\big)^{\dagger}=(e_{F^{\dagger}}\circ G)\bullet(F\circ\alpha^{\dagger}\circ G)\bullet(F\circ\iota_{G^{\dagger}})

we can deduce the planar-pivotal pre-adjunction datum

e_{F}^{\dagger}=\iota_{F^{\dagger}},\qquad\iota_{F}^{\dagger}=e_{F^{\dagger}}

on the hom-categories.

An immediate consequence of this is the following. Taking
\alpha=\operatorname{id}_{F}
to be the identity natural transformation on an endofunctor
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}
, then we have the following
adjunction-snake equations

\operatorname{id}_{F}=(e_{F}\circ F^{\dagger})\bullet(F^{\dagger}\circ\iota_{F}),\qquad\operatorname{id}_{\,{}^{\dagger}F}=(\,^{\dagger}F\circ{e}_{F})\bullet({\iota}_{F}\circ\,^{\dagger}F).

Furthermore, since the adjunction is involutive, the above proposition as well as planar-unitarity implies

\iota_{F}^{\dagger}=e_{F^{\dagger}},\qquad e_{F}^{\dagger}=\iota_{F^{\dagger}}.

This is a part of the condition of pivotality for 1-categories
[
BW99
]
.

3.2.3
Adjunctions of the tensor product

Recall that the tensor product of (planar-unitary) 2-representations are determined by the coproduct functor
\tilde{\Delta}
on
\tilde{C}
. Though orientation reversal is contravariant on
\tilde{C}
, the fact that it does
not
land in the comonoidal-opposite means the following

({\cal D}\boxtimes F^{\prime})^{\dagger}={\cal D}\boxtimes F^{\prime\dagger},\qquad(F\boxtimes{\cal A})^{\dagger}=F^{\dagger}\boxtimes{\cal A}

for each
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}
and
F^{\prime}:{\cal A}\rightarrow{\cal A}^{\prime}
. Moreover, the fact that the identity endofunctor
1_{\cal D}
has equipped the trivial
{\cal E}
-structure given by the counit/identity arrow
\tilde{\epsilon}_{1}(a_{v})={\bf 1}_{v}
gives

\displaystyle e_{{\cal D}\boxtimes F^{\prime}}=1_{\cal D}\boxtimes e_{F^{\prime}},\qquad e_{F\boxtimes{\cal A}}=e_{F}\boxtimes 1_{{\cal A}}

\displaystyle\iota_{{\cal D}\boxtimes F^{\prime}}=1_{\cal D}\boxtimes\iota_{F^{\prime}},\qquad\iota_{F\boxtimes{\cal A}}=\iota_{F}\boxtimes 1_{{\cal A}}.

From Definition of 2.2.3 of
[
DR18
]
, we thus have the following.

Theorem 3.5
.

The
planar-unitary
2-representations form a
planar-pivotal
monoidal 2-category
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
.

In other words, planar-unitarity implies planar-pivotality, meaning that the end-categories of
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
are themselves pivotal monoidal. To make a planar-pivotal 2-category bona fide
pivotal
,
[
DR18
]
introduced a notion of object-level duality satisfying several further coherence axioms. In the following, we shall do the same by using the antipode functor
\tilde{S}
, but we will see that we in fact do
not
produce a pivotal 2-category.

3.3
Duality of 2-representations

We now turn to the (object-level) duality in
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
. We shall introduce the notion of a
left/right-dual
through the antipode functor
\tilde{S}
,
7
7

7

The functor
\tilde{S}^{-1}
is interpreted as both left- and right-adjoint to
\tilde{S}
, witnessed by natural transformations

\tilde{S}\circ\tilde{S}^{-1}\Rightarrow 1_{\tilde{C}}\Leftarrow\tilde{S}^{-1}\circ\tilde{S}.

\rho^{*}(\zeta)=\rho(\tilde{S}\zeta),\qquad\,^{*}\rho(\zeta)=\rho(\tilde{S}^{-1}\zeta),\qquad\forall\penalty\ \zeta\in\tilde{C}.

To express this definition more explicitly in terms of the action functor
\rhd
, we define the restrictions

\tilde{S}\mid_{{\cal V}}=\tilde{S}_{0},\qquad\tilde{S}\mid_{{\cal E}}=\tilde{S}_{1}.

We can then define for each functor
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}
the following left- and right-
mates
,

\displaystyle\zeta\rhd({\cal D}^{\prime*}\xrightarrow{F^{*}}{\cal D}^{*})=(\tilde{S}_{0}a_{v}^{\prime})\rhd{\cal D}^{\prime*}\xrightarrow{\tilde{S}_{1}\gamma_{e}\rhd F^{*}}(\tilde{S}_{0}a_{v})\rhd{\cal D}^{*},

\displaystyle\zeta\rhd(\,^{*}{\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\,{}^{*}F}\,^{*}{\cal D})=(\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}a_{v}^{\prime})\rhd\,^{*}{\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{e}\rhd\,^{*}F}(\tilde{S}_{0}^{-1}a_{v})\rhd\,^{*}{\cal D},

which inherits the
{\cal E}
-structure given by
\tilde{S}_{1}\gamma_{e}\rhd-
from that of the functor
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}
under
a_{v}
.

Note that, in writing "
\tilde{S}_{1}^{-1}
" here, we are implicitly using the "fully-cofaithful-ness" of
\tilde{S}
; that is, it is bijective on arrows. Since, similar to orientation reversal,
\tilde{S}
swaps the sources and targets on arrows, the left- and right-mates behaves in the following way

\displaystyle({\cal D}\xrightarrow{F}{\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{G}{\cal D}^{\prime\prime})^{*}=({\cal D}^{\prime\prime*}\xrightarrow{G^{*}}{\cal D}^{\prime*}\xrightarrow{F^{*}}{\cal D}^{*}),\qquad(F\xRightarrow{\alpha}F^{\prime})^{*}=(F^{*}\xRightarrow{\alpha^{*}}F^{\prime*})

\,{}^{*}({\cal D}\xrightarrow{F}{\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{G}{\cal D}^{\prime\prime})=(\,^{*}{\cal D}^{\prime\prime}\xrightarrow{\,{}^{*}G}\,^{*}{\cal D}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\,{}^{*}F}\,^{*}{\cal D}),\qquad\,^{*}(F\xRightarrow{\alpha}F^{\prime})=(\,^{*}F\xRightarrow{\,{}^{*}\alpha}\,^{*}F^{\prime})

with respect to composition and the naturality of in the hom-categories.

Remark 3.3
.

By the hypothesis that
\tilde{S}
is an equivalence, we have in fact from the definition that
\,{}^{*}{\cal D}^{*}\cong{\cal D}
, so the right-dual can be thought of as the "pre-left-dual". However,
\tilde{S}
is in general not going to be unipotent
\tilde{S}^{2}\not\simeq 1_{\tilde{C}}
, hence neither the left- nor right-dualities are involutive, eg.
({\cal D}^{*})^{*}\not\cong{\cal D}
. As such, without assuming additional "pivotality conditions", our 2-category
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
cannot be bona fide pivotal, and must differ in certain respects from similar structures studied in the literature (eg.
[
DR18
,
JR23
,
Mac00
]
). We will discuss this in more detail later.

\Diamond

However, unlike orientation reversal,
\tilde{S}:\tilde{C}\rightarrow\tilde{C}^{\text{m-op,c-op}}
(and its inverse) lands in the comonoidal opposite. This allows us to deduce the compatibility of mates against the tensor products which is different from the adjoints. To see this, we first note the fact that
\tilde{S}_{1}
preserves the identity arrows in
\tilde{C}
. This then implies that the left- and right-dual preserves the identity functors for all
{\cal A}
,

1_{{\cal A}^{*}}=1_{{\cal A}}^{*},\qquad 1_{\,{}^{*}{\cal A}}=\,^{*}1_{\cal A},

(3.10)

since both sides of each equations above have the same
\tilde{C}
-module structure (under
{\cal V}
). Thanks to this, we have achieve

\displaystyle({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}\xrightarrow{F\boxtimes{\cal A}}{\cal D}^{\prime}\boxtimes{\cal A})^{*}

\displaystyle={\cal A}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{\prime*}\xrightarrow{{\cal A}^{*}\boxtimes F^{*}}{\cal A}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*}

\displaystyle({\cal A}\boxtimes{\cal D}\xrightarrow{{\cal A}\boxtimes F}{\cal A}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{\prime})^{*}

\displaystyle={\cal D}^{\prime*}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{*}\xrightarrow{F^{*}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{*}}{\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{*}

for each
\tilde{C}
-module category
{\cal A}
and functors
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{\prime}
. Similarly for the right-dual, as the inverse
\tilde{S}^{-1}
works the same way. Note the condition
\tilde{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{S}=\tilde{\epsilon}=\tilde{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{S}^{-1}
implies that
{\cal I}={\cal I}^{*}=\,^{*}{\cal I}
on the nose.

3.3.1
Folds for the duals

Now take a
\tilde{C}
-module category
{\cal D}
and an endofunctor
F\in\operatorname{End}({\cal D})
. Define the functor
F^{*}\boxtimes F:{\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D}
by nudging. The following tensor product object-functor pairs admit the following
\tilde{C}
-module structure

\displaystyle\zeta\rhd({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D}\xrightarrow{F^{*}\boxtimes F}{\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=(\tilde{S}_{0}\otimes 1)(\tilde{\Delta}_{0})_{a_{v}}\rhd({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D})\xrightarrow{(\tilde{S}_{1}\otimes 1)(\tilde{\Delta}_{1})_{\gamma_{e}}\rhd(F^{*}\boxtimes F)}(\tilde{S}_{0}\otimes 1)(\tilde{\Delta}_{0})_{a_{v}^{\prime}}\rhd({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D}),

\displaystyle\zeta\rhd({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*}\xrightarrow{F\boxtimes F^{*}}{\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=(1\otimes\tilde{S}_{0})(\tilde{\Delta}_{0})_{a_{v}}\rhd({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})\xrightarrow{(1\otimes\tilde{S}_{1})(\tilde{\Delta}_{1})_{\gamma_{e}}\rhd(F\boxtimes F^{*})}(1\otimes\tilde{S}_{0})(\tilde{\Delta}_{0})_{a_{v}^{\prime}}\rhd({\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*}),

where
\zeta=a_{v}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{e}}a_{v}^{\prime}\in\tilde{C}
.

Now since the above action functors
\rho=\rhd
are the same (they are all those of
{\cal D}
), the module associator
(\rho(-)\otimes\rho(-))(-)\Rightarrow\rho(-\cdot-)(-)
allows us to contract the 2-gauge transformations. The antipode axioms (
2.2
) then tell us that this the above
\tilde{C}
-module structures on
{\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D}
, etc. are the same as that for
{\cal I}
, along with its identity endomorphism
1_{\cal I}
. This is witnessed by
\tilde{C}
-module functors,

\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}:{\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal I},\qquad\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}:{\cal I}\rightarrow{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*},

called the
right-folds
, which fit into the following naturality diagrams,

for each

functor
F:{\cal D}\rightarrow{\cal D}

\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\circ(F^{*}\boxtimes{\cal D})\Rightarrow\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\circ({{\cal D}^{*}}\boxtimes F),\qquad(F\boxtimes{{\cal D}^{*}})\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\Rightarrow({{\cal D}}\boxtimes F)\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D},

which satisfy the coherence condition
