{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 100.65 -53.02 h 12.82 v 12.68 h -12.82 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{74.89166pt}{-36.16661pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 103.63 -50.04)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the central triangle is filled by
c_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D},\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}}\bullet\bar{K}_{\cal D}^{\prime}
, and the square to its right is
{\upsilon_{\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D},c_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},{\cal D}}}}
.

Now starting with the functor

\big({\cal D}\boxtimes(\vartheta_{\cal D}\circ\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D})\big)\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}:{\cal I}\rightarrow{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D},

we can apply a series of 2-morphisms

\displaystyle\big({\cal D}\boxtimes(\vartheta_{\cal D}\circ\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D})\big)\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{({\cal D}\boxtimes\vartheta_{\cal D})\circ\mathrm{P}_{\cal D}}({\cal D}\boxtimes\vartheta_{\cal D})\circ c_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},{\cal D}}\circ\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{c_{{\cal D},\vartheta_{\cal D}}\circ\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}}c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}\circ(\vartheta_{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D})\circ\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}\xRightarrow{c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}\circ\mathrm{Q}_{\cal D}}c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}\circ c_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}

\displaystyle\cong c_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}^{\dagger}\circ c_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\xRightarrow{e_{c_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}}\circ\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}}\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D},

where in the last line we have used the quasi-Hermimticity property
c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}\cong c_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}^{\dagger}
. This defines
\kappa_{\cal D}
; the other one
\bar{\kappa}_{\cal D}
can be obtained similarly.
∎

The belt-buckle moves
\mathrm{P},\mathrm{Q}
are also useful for transporting the rigidity conditions introduced in §
5.3.1
, §
5.3.2
to the ribbon balancings.

There is a priori no reason for the different twist types to be related. Indeed, in the geometric picture, the
2\pi
-rotations of the framing introduced by the left-under
\vartheta_{\cal D}^{\dagger}
and right-under
\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}
balancings are related by a reflection about the vertical line. However, even if the two ribbon balancings do coincide, it still does not mean that they can be trivialized individually. This brings us to the first Reidemseister move mentioned in
Remark
5.3
.

6.1.3
Reidemeister I: half-framed, unframed and self-dual objects

We now prove in this section that, under certain conditions, we can find invertible 2-morphisms which witness the
first Reidemeister moves
. However, due to the intricate duality structures involved, there are subtleties associated to the constructions — specifically, we will construct
two
different Reidemeister I witnesses in the following.

For this section, we will assume
\tilde{R}
is quasi-Hermitian (
5.4
), and that the fold-crossing 2-morphisms
H,G
and the writhes
K,\bar{K}
, as defined in §
5.2.2
, are invertible. We now seek to construct two related, but a priori distinct, versions of the Reidemeister I move. These are invertible 2-morphisms

\mathrm{R}_{\cal D}:\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}\Rightarrow(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}),\qquad\mathrm{L}_{\cal D}:(\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})\circ(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D})\Rightarrow\vartheta_{\cal D}.

Note crucially that quasi-Hermiticity does
not
imply
c_{{\cal D}^{*},\,^{*}{\cal D}}^{\dagger}\cong c_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}
!

1.

Definition of
\mathrm{R}_{\cal D}
:
by definition,

\displaystyle\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}

\displaystyle=(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D})\circ(c_{{\cal D}^{*},\,^{*}{\cal D}}\boxtimes{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D})

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{G_{{\cal D},\,^{*}{\cal D}}^{-1}\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D})}(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes c_{{\cal D},\,^{*}{\cal D}}^{\dagger})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D})

\displaystyle\qquad\cong(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ\big({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes(c_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},{\cal D}}\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}})\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes K_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}})}(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}),

where in the third line we have used quasi-Hermiticity
c_{{\cal D},\,^{*}{\cal D}}^{\dagger}\cong c_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},{\cal D}}
, and the equivalences described in
Remark
3.4
.

2.

Definition of
\mathrm{L}_{\cal D}
:
we begin instead with the adjoint
\vartheta_{\cal D}^{\dagger}
. Then,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\cal D}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}\boxtimes c_{{\cal D}^{*},\,^{*}{\cal D}}^{\dagger})\circ(\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{H_{{\cal D},\,^{*}{\cal D}}\circ(\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})}(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\circ(c_{{\cal D},\,^{*}{\cal D}}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})\circ(\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad\cong(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\circ\big((c_{{\cal D},\,^{*}{\cal D}}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}}^{\dagger})\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\xRightarrow{(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\circ(\bar{K}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}}^{-1}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})}(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\circ(\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*}),

whence taking the adjunction-mate yields the desired 2-morphism
\mathrm{L}_{\cal D}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

There exist
double-twist cancellations of the second type

m_{\cal D}:\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}\circ\vartheta_{\cal D}\Rightarrow 1_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}},\qquad n_{\cal D}:1_{{\cal D}^{*}}\Rightarrow\vartheta_{\cal D}\circ\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}

as mentioned previously in §
6.1.2
.

These are "off-the-fold" versions of
\kappa_{\cal D},\bar{\kappa}_{\cal D}
. To see how they are related, see §
6.1.4
.

Proof.

Consider the composite
\mathrm{R}\circ\mathrm{L}^{-1}
. Under the hypothesis of the previous proposition,
\mathrm{R}\circ\mathrm{L}^{-1}
is a(n invertible) 2-morphism

\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}\circ\vartheta_{\cal D}\Rightarrow(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\circ(\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})\circ(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}).

By
Remark
3.4
, we can write
\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}\cong\operatorname{ev}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}}\cong\,^{*}\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}
, such that the functor on the right-hand side reads

(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes\,^{*}\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\circ(\,^{*}\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})\circ(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}),

(6.5)

Now by a series of interchangers, we can bring the barred-folds to the outside such that

(\,^{*}{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ\Big(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\big(({\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D})\circ(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D})\big)\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D}\Big)\circ(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}),

whence it becomes clear that it admits a tuple of snakerators/cusps

\displaystyle(\,^{*}{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ\Big(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\big(({\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D})\circ(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D})\big)\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D}\Big)\circ(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\xRightarrow{(\,^{*}{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ\Big(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\varphi_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D}\Big)\circ(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})}(\,^{*}{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\xRightarrow{\,{}^{*}\varphi_{\cal D}^{\dagger}}1_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}}.

This allows us to directly trivialize the double twist; similar argument applies to
\mathrm{L}\circ\mathrm{R}^{-1}
, whence

\displaystyle m_{\cal D}=\,^{*}\varphi_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\bullet(\,^{*}{\cal D}\boxtimes\varphi_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\bullet(\mathrm{R}\circ\mathrm{L}^{-1}):\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}\circ\vartheta_{\cal D}\Rightarrow 1_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}}.

\displaystyle n_{\cal D}:(\mathrm{L}\circ\mathrm{R}^{-1})\bullet({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes\,^{*}\varrho_{{\cal D}}^{\dagger}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})\bullet\varrho_{\cal D}:1_{{\cal D}^{*}}\Rightarrow\vartheta_{\cal D}\circ\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}.

(6.6)

∎

More refined notions of "framing" (cf.
[
BL03
,
DR18
,
BMS24
]
) can thus be achieved.

Definition 6.5
.

Take
{\cal D}\in\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
and suppose the 2-Drinfel’d modifications
\omega_{\cal D},\bar{\omega}_{\cal D}
are invertible.

1.

{\cal D}
is
half-framed
if we have (i) an identification
\vartheta_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\cong\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}
, and (ii) the two types of Kaufmann double twist cancellations coincide,

m_{\cal D}=e_{\vartheta_{\cal D}},\qquad n_{\cal D}=\iota_{\vartheta_{\cal D}}.

(6.7)

2.

a half-framed
{\cal D}
is
unframed
iff (i) its left- and right-duals coincide
\,{}^{*}{\cal D}\cong{\cal D}^{*}
such that (
4.4
) holds, and (ii) we have an identification
\vartheta_{\cal D}\cong\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}
such that
\mathrm{L}^{-1}_{\cal D}\bullet\mathrm{R}_{\cal D}
is a 2-isomorphism on
\vartheta_{\cal D}
.

3.

a unframed
{\cal D}
is
self-dual
iff (i)
{\cal D}^{*}\cong{\cal D}
and we have the identifications

\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\cong\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger},\qquad\varphi_{\cal D}=\varrho_{\cal D}^{\dagger},

and (ii)
\vartheta_{\cal D}=\vartheta_{\cal D}^{*}
and
\mathrm{R}_{\cal D}=\mathrm{L}_{\cal D}^{-1}
.

The notion of "unframed" defined above is justified as follows. From
Remark
4.2
, we see that the barred-folds coincide with the regular folds
\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}\cong\operatorname{ev}_{{\cal D}^{*}}
whenever the dual is involutive
{\cal D}^{**}\cong{\cal D}
. If (
4.4
) further holds, then the functor
(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\cong(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes{\operatorname{cev}}_{{\cal D}^{**}})
admits a snakerator
\varrho_{{\cal D}}
from
1_{{\cal D}^{*}}
. Composing its with
\mathrm{R}_{\cal D}
then kills a single twist

\varrho_{{\cal D}}^{\dagger}\bullet\mathrm{R}_{\cal D}:\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}\cong\vartheta_{\cal D}\Rightarrow 1_{{\cal D}^{*}};

(6.8)

similar arguments gives a trivialization of
\vartheta_{\cal D}^{\dagger}
from
\mathrm{L}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}
. Having these witnesses for a single Reidemeister I move is what motivates the above definition of "unframed object".

Remark 6.5
.

Let us take a closer look at the "double strange snakerators/cusps" (
6.5
). Diagrammatically, they bear a striking resemblance to fig. 30 in
[
BMS24
]
, and the reason is as follows. Suppose the double-dual
-^{**}
defines an involution
-^{\#}
in accordance with
Remark
6.3
, then the barred folds can intuitively be understood as the double-duality datum. The condition (
4.1
) then gives a
3
-endomorphism

\Gamma_{\text{pt}}:\text{pt}^{\#\dagger\#\dagger}\Rrightarrow\text{pt}

in the monoidal 3-category
B\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
. Together with the involutive-ness of the adjunction
\Theta_{\text{pt}}:\text{pt}^{\dagger\dagger}\Rrightarrow\text{pt}
, we achieve precisely
Theorem 4.5
in
[
BMS24
]
. Moreover, the "half-framing" condition (
6.7
) identifies the two types of Kaufmann double twist cancellations, such that a correspondence with the structures in a
\mathsf{Gray}
-category with duals can be made. This substantiates the contents of Table
1
.

\Diamond

The notions of framing introduced in
Definition
6.5
detect the geometry of a surface labelled by
{\cal D}\in\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
through its conditions on the ribbon twists. The situation can be summarized in the following table
2
.

{\cal D}
is…

fully-framed

half-framed

unframed

self-dual

distinct twists

\vartheta_{\cal D},\vartheta_{\cal D}^{*},\vartheta_{\cal D}^{\dagger}

\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D},\,^{*}\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D},\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}

\vartheta_{\cal D},\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}

\vartheta_{\cal D}^{*},\,^{*}\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}

\vartheta_{\cal D},\vartheta_{\cal D}^{*}

\vartheta_{\cal D}

Table 2:
A table describing the number of twists on an object
{\cal D}
depending on how framed it is. Notice twists such as
\,{}^{*}\vartheta_{\cal D},\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D}^{*}
did not appear due to the invertibility of the 2-Drinfel’d modifications, which relate these back to
\bar{\vartheta},\vartheta
respectively.

The following relations
\vartheta_{\cal D}^{**}\cong\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D},\,^{**}\bar{\vartheta}\cong\vartheta_{\cal D}
between the ribbon balancings can be seen from the triviality of the quadruple dual. Indeed, according to
Remark
6.3
, double-duals can be morally replaced by "barred" quantities.

6.1.4
Unframed self-dual objects

We now turn to the notion of "self-dual" objects. If
{\cal D}
were self-dual, then its Reidemeister I moves in
Proposition
6.4
coincide. Moreover, under quasi-Hermiticity, one also sees that the coherent writhings
K_{\cal D}=\bar{K}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}
on
{\cal D}
coincide (see §
5.3.1
). A self-dual object is thus one which is equipped with (i) a single coherent writhing, and (ii) a
single
notion of twist and a Reidemsiter I move trivializing it. This is very close to what an "unframed self-dual object"
{\cal D}
means in
[
BL03
]
, but we require one more reduction.

Recall from §
6.1.2
that, through a belt-buckle move, we can apply a writhing to remove a single twist on a fold. As such, the 2-morphisms

K_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}}\bullet\mathrm{P}_{\cal D}:({\cal D}\boxtimes\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D})\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\Rightarrow\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger},\qquad K_{\cal D}\bullet\mathrm{Q}_{\cal D}:(\vartheta_{{\cal D}}\boxtimes{\cal D})\circ\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}\Rightarrow\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}

should be closely related to the first Reidemeister moves (
6.4
). Particularly in the
unframed
case, a 2-morphism parallel with
K_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}}\bullet\mathrm{P}_{\cal D}
can be constructed from (
6.8
),

({\cal D}\boxtimes\varrho_{{\cal D}}^{*}\bullet\mathrm{R}_{\cal D})\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}:({\cal D}\boxtimes\bar{\vartheta}_{\cal D})\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\Rightarrow\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\cong\widetilde{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}\cong\overline{\operatorname{ev}}^{\dagger}_{\cal D},

whence the condition stating that these moves are equivalent,

({\cal D}\boxtimes\varrho_{{\cal D}}^{*}\bullet\mathrm{R}_{\cal D})\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}=K_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}}\bullet\mathrm{P}_{\cal D},

(6.9)

is the precise way to phrase the "writhes = first Reidemeister move" perspective taken in
[
BL03
]
(see also
Remark
5.3
).

By applying (
6.9
) twice, one can immediately deduce that
\kappa_{\cal D}
coincides with
m_{\cal D}
in (
6.6
). The half-framing condition (
6.7
) then tells us that the two types of twist cancellations coincide on a fold,

\kappa_{\cal D}=({\cal D}\boxtimes e_{\vartheta_{\cal D}})\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}.

This finally allows us to recover the notion of a "unframed self-dual generator" in
[
BL03
]
.

6.2
Double braiding; the Hopf links

Let us now consider the
double braiding
map
C_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}=c_{{\cal A},{\cal D}}\circ c_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}:{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}\rightarrow{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}
mentioned in
Remark
5.1
. It is an endofunctor, hence we are able to form the duality folds on them,

\operatorname{ev}_{C_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}}:C_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}^{*}\boxtimes C_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{1_{\cal I}},\qquad\operatorname{cev}_{C_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}}:\operatorname{id}_{1_{\cal I}}\Rightarrow C_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}\boxtimes C_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}^{*}.

They witness the null-homotopy of adjacent double braidings — and hence Hopf links; see the following — with the opposite framing.

6.2.1
Hopf links on distinct objects; the 2-Hopf modifications

Let us first begin with a brief survey on the Hopf link 1-morphisms we can construct on distinct objects
{\cal D},{\cal A}\in\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
. For each pair of objects
{\cal D},{\cal A}
and endomorphisms
F\in\operatorname{End}({\cal D}),G\in\operatorname{End}({\cal A})
, we construct the following 1- and 2-morphisms

\displaystyle h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}=(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal A}^{\dagger})\circ({\cal D}\boxtimes C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal A}}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{*})\circ(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal A}):{\cal I}\rightarrow{\cal I},

\displaystyle h_{F,{\cal A}}=(\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{F^{*}}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal A}^{\dagger})\circ(F\boxtimes C_{F^{*},{\cal A}}\boxtimes{\cal A}^{*})\circ(\operatorname{cev}_{F}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal A}):h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}\Rightarrow h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}},

\displaystyle h_{{\cal D},G}=(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\widetilde{\operatorname{ev}}_{G*})\circ({\cal D}\boxtimes C_{{\cal D}^{*},G}\boxtimes G^{*})\circ(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{G}):h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}\Rightarrow h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}.

As the mixed double braiding 2-morphisms is compatible with nudging, we have

\upsilon_{F\boxtimes F^{*},G\boxtimes G^{*}}\bullet(h_{F,{\cal A}}\bullet h_{{\cal D},G})\bullet\upsilon_{F\boxtimes F^{*},G\boxtimes G^{*}}^{-1}=h_{{\cal D},G}\bullet h_{F,{\cal A}},

which means that the matrix elements of the "mixed Hopf links"
h_{F,{\cal A}},h_{{\cal D},G}
commute up to the invertible interchanger
\upsilon
. Hence, if we fix a basis for which the interchangers are diagonal, the matrices corresponding to the mixed Hopf links can be simultaneously diagonalized.

We now consider the relationship between the Hopf links
h
and its close relative.

Theorem 6.6
.

Define the
\mathrm{pivotal\penalty\ opposite}
of
h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}
,

\tilde{h}_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}=({\operatorname{ev}}_{{\cal D}}\boxtimes{\operatorname{ev}}_{{\cal A}})\circ({\cal D}^{*}\boxtimes C_{{\cal D},{\cal A}^{*}}\boxtimes{\cal A})\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\operatorname{ev}_{\cal A}^{\dagger}):{\cal I}\rightarrow{\cal I}.

Assume the 2-morphisms in
Proposition
3.7
are invertible, then there are 2-morphisms such that

h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}\Rightarrow\tilde{h}_{{\cal D},{\cal A}},\qquad\,^{*}h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}\Rightarrow\tilde{h}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal A},\,^{*}{\cal D}}.

Hence, there is a 2-morphism
\tilde{h}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},\,^{*}{\cal A}}^{*}\Rightarrow\tilde{h}_{{\cal A},{\cal D}}
which swaps the arguments of the Hopf links under an orientation reversal.

Proof.

By using
c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal A}}
, its unitary hexagonators and the braid exchange (
3.3
), we can form the following 2-morphism
11
11

11

Note that this 2-morphism acts like an interchanger, but it is
not
one: it interchanges two functors which act on overlapping objects.

(6.10)

where we have neglected the obvious arguments in
\Omega
to save space. Notice if
{\cal A}={\cal D}
, then the diamond in the middle of (
6.10
) is related to the interchangers that have appeared in (
5.11
), and therefore plays a part in the swallowking equations (
5.12
) in §
5.3.2
. This observation will be crucial later in §
6.2.3
.

Together with the following adjunction described in §

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{73.1535pt}{-8.85591pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 101.22 -12.25)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}:h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}\Rightarrow\tilde{h}_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}

which proves the first part.

The second part will use the invertibility of the 2-morphisms
a_{\cal D},b_{\cal D}
in
Proposition
3.7
, as well as the duality transforms (
6.3
)
\,{}^{*}C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal A}}\cong C_{\,{}^{*}{\cal A},{\cal D}}.
We have that

\,{}^{*}h_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}

\displaystyle\cong(\,^{*}\operatorname{cev}_{\cal A}\boxtimes\,^{*}\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D})\circ({\cal A}\boxtimes\,^{*}C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal A}}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ(\,^{*}\operatorname{cev}_{\cal A}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\,^{*}\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\big(b_{\cal A}^{-1}\boxtimes b_{\cal D}^{-1}\big)\circ({\cal A}\boxtimes\cong\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ\big(b_{\cal A}^{\dagger}\boxtimes b_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\big)}(\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal A}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D})\circ({\cal A}\boxtimes C_{\,{}^{*}{\cal A},{\cal D}}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ(\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal A}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\cong(\operatorname{ev}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal A}}\boxtimes\operatorname{ev}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}})\circ({\cal A}\boxtimes C_{\,{}^{*}{\cal A},{\cal D}}\boxtimes\,^{*}{\cal D})\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal A}}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\operatorname{ev}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D}}^{\dagger})=\tilde{h}_{\,{}^{*}{\cal A},\,^{*}{\cal D}},

where we have used
Remark
3.4
in the final line. Composing the dual of this 2-morphisms with
\Gamma_{{\cal D},{\cal A}}
then proves the theorem.
∎

We note here that it is in general not possible to swap the arguments of the Hopf links without an orientation reversal being performed — that is, unless the double braiding on
{\cal D}\boxtimes{\cal A}
happens to be trivializable. We shall see that this occurs precisely in the special case
{\cal A}={\cal D}
; let us explore this in the following.

6.2.2
Trivializable false Hopf links

Letting now
{\cal A}={\cal D}
, consider the following
"false"
({\cal D}^{*},{\cal D})
-Hopf link

h_{{\cal D}}=h_{{\cal D},{\cal D}}=(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\circ({\cal D}\boxtimes C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}\boxtimes{\cal D}^{*})\circ(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}):{\cal I}\rightarrow{\cal I}.

By quasi-Hermiticity (
5.4
), the double-braiding is self-dual
C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}^{\dagger}\cong C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}
. The adjoint-inverse of (
5.5
),
(k_{\cal D}^{\dagger})^{-1}:\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}\circ C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}\Rightarrow\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}
,
allows us to construct a 2-morphism
\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}
trivializing the
({\cal D}^{*},{\cal D})
-Hopf link, given by the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cal I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cal I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\cal I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(k_{\cal D}^{\dagger})^{-1}}

where the triangles on the sides are given by the tensoring of appropriate folds with the snakerators/cusps
\varphi_{\cal D},{\varphi}^{\dagger}
. This 2-morphism trivializes a Hopf linking involving
{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}
; similarly for the left-dual
\,{}^{*}{\cal D}
. We call these "false" Hopf links, as they can be disentangled.

Focusing on the
({\cal D},{\cal D}^{*})
-Hopf link and its trivialization
\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}
, there is a another trivialization
\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}^{\prime}:1_{\cal I}\Rightarrow h_{{\cal D}}
given by the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cal I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cal I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}{\cal D}{\cal D}^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{\cal I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{\cal D}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}}}

constructed from the snakerators/cusps
\varrho^{\dagger},\varrho^{\dagger}
, instead of
\varphi
. The same arguments hold for the left-dual versions of the Hopf links, by making use of the barred-versions of the folds and snakerators/cusps.

6.2.3
False Hopf links and the swallowking equation

In light of quasi-Hermiticity
c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}^{\dagger}\cong c_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}
and the relation
\iota_{F}^{\dagger}=e_{F^{\dagger}}
for the adjunction-folds, the middle square in
\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}^{\prime}
is adjoint to that in
\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}
. However, the two triangles at the sides are different:
\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}
has
\varphi
’s while
\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}^{\prime}
has
\varrho
’s. They are, of course, related through the swallowtail 2-morphisms
\mathcal{S}_{\cal D},\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}^{\prime}
studied in §
3.3.4
; concisely, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\prime\dagger}_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}=\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}\circ\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}^{\dagger},\qquad\mathcal{H}^{\prime\dagger}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\cal D}\circ\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}^{\prime\dagger}.

(6.11)

Here we have kept the 2-morphisms
a,b
implicit, but they can be easily written in.

Now on the other hand, (
3.3
) and the ensuing diagram
\Gamma_{{\cal D},{\cal D}}
there allow us to exhibit a 2-morphism

\Lambda_{\cal D}=\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}\bullet\Gamma_{{\cal D},{\cal D}}\bullet\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}:1_{\cal I}\Rightarrow 1_{\cal I}

which relates
\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}
with
\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}
,

\Lambda_{\cal D}=\hbox to45.51pt{\vbox to87.84pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.75394pt\lower-43.92058pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

where
\bar{k}_{\cal D}^{-1}:C_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}\circ\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\Rightarrow\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}
is the inverse of (
5.7
).

Proposition 6.7
.

The conditions (
6.11
) and the swallowking equations (
5.12
) imply that
\Lambda_{\cal D}
is self-adjoint

\Lambda_{\cal D}=\Lambda_{\cal D}^{\dagger}.

Note quasi-Hermiticity
c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}^{\dagger}\cong c_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}
implies that we have the contraction

\operatorname{id}_{c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}}\circ\operatorname{id}^{\dagger}_{c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}}=\operatorname{id}_{c_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}}\circ\operatorname{id}_{c_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}}=\operatorname{id}_{C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}}.

Proof.

Recall the observation made below (
6.10
). If we paste the above expression for
\Lambda_{\cal D}
with the swallowtails
\mathcal{S}_{\cal D},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\cal D}
and their adjoints on either side, this observation braid exchange square in the middle of
\Gamma_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}
fits precisely in between the 2-morphism
\mathcal{K}_{\cal D}
obtained in §
5.3.2
,

(\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}\circ\mathcal{K}_{\cal D}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}^{\prime}),\qquad(\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{K}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}^{\prime\dagger}),

where we recall
\mathcal{K}_{\cal D}
is obtained by gluing the big diamond (
5.11
) with (
5.10
). This allows us to form a 2-morphism expressed schematically by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}_{\cal D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma_{{\cal D},{\cal D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{K}^{\dagger}_{\cal D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\cal D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}^{\prime\dagger}_{\cal D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

However, (
6.11
) states that this should be nothing but
\Lambda^{\dagger}_{\cal D}
. The statement then follows from the swallowking (
5.12
).
∎

If
\tilde{R}
were Hermitian instead of just quasi-Hermitian, then by
Remark
5.1
it would mean the entire 2-category is sylleptic, hence all Hopf links are false. Thus, one should read the above proposition as a
coherence condition
imposed on sylleptic 2-categories with duals.

6.2.4
Order of the Hopf links

In light of the above discussion, we now turn to studying the non-trivializable
"true" Hopf links
on
{\cal D}
. They are given by the following endofunctors on
{\cal I}
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\cal D}=(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\circ({\cal D}\boxtimes C_{{\cal D}^{*},\,^{*}{\cal D}}\boxtimes{\cal D})\circ(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}),

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}_{\cal D}=(\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}\boxtimes{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D})\circ({\cal D}\boxtimes C_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}\boxtimes{\cal D})\circ(\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle\beta_{\cal D}=(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\operatorname{ev}_{{\cal D}})\circ({\cal D}\boxtimes C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}^{*}}\boxtimes{\cal D})\circ(\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}),

\displaystyle\bar{\beta}_{\cal D}=(\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger})\circ({\cal D}\boxtimes C_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},\,^{*}{\cal D}}\boxtimes{\cal D})\circ(\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}^{\dagger}\boxtimes\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}).

Other Hopf links can be obtained by applying interchangers to swap the order of the duality folds. Since each of their composites respects the tensor product, so do they.

Recall form
Definition
6.5
that being "half-framed"
\vartheta^{\dagger}\cong\bar{\vartheta}
relates the ribbon balancings to their adjoints. For the Hopf links, on the other hand, a similar condition between the
\alpha,\beta
’s has less to do with the framing, but rather the order of its double braiding.

Proposition 6.8
.

Consider the following objects in
\tilde{C}
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}_{\alpha}=(-\cdot-)\big((\tilde{S}^{4}\otimes 1)(\tilde{R}\penalty\ \hat{\cdot}\penalty\ \tilde{R})\big),\qquad\mathfrak{r}_{\bar{\alpha}}=(-\cdot-)\big((1\otimes\tilde{S}^{4})(\tilde{R}\penalty\ \hat{\cdot}\penalty\ \tilde{R})\big),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{r}_{\beta}=\tilde{S}^{4}(-\cdot-)(\tilde{R}\penalty\ \hat{\cdot}\penalty\ \tilde{R}),\qquad\mathfrak{r}_{\bar{\beta}}=\tilde{S}^{-4}(-\cdot-)(\tilde{R}\penalty\ \hat{\cdot}\penalty\ \tilde{R}).

For each
\chi=\alpha,\bar{\alpha},\beta,\bar{\beta}
, if
\overline{\mathfrak{r}}_{\chi}=\mathfrak{r}_{\chi}^{T}
is Hermitian (cf. (
5.4
)) then the true Hopf link
\chi_{\cal D}
is self-adjoint.

Proof.

The key observation is that the double braidings in the definition of
\alpha,\bar{\alpha}
involves objects that are two (pre-)duals apart, while those in
\beta,\bar{\beta}
involves objects with the same number of (pre-)duals. Since
\tilde{S}^{2}
is a monoidal functor, the quantities
\mathfrak{r}_{\chi}
can be seen to implement these double braiding maps in the true Hopf link
\chi_{\cal D}
for each
\chi=\alpha,\bar{\alpha},\beta,\bar{\beta}
. Thus, under (
6.3
) and
Remark
5.1
, the Hermiticity of
\mathfrak{r}_{\chi}
then implies that these double braids are self-adjoint. The proposition then follows directly.

∎

The self-adojointedness of the Hopf links gives a trivialization of its square,

e_{\alpha_{\cal D}}:\alpha_{\cal D}\circ\alpha_{\cal D}\Rightarrow 1_{\cal I};

similarly for the other Hopf links.

In general, the number
n<\infty
at which the
n
-fold Hopf links
12
12

12

Note these are the Hopf links constructed from
n
-hold compositions of the double braiding
C_{{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}}^{\circ n}
, not the
n
-fold composition of the Hopf links themselves.

\chi_{n,{\cal D}}^{\dagger}\cong\chi_{n,{\cal D}}
become self-adjoint can be deduced from the order
n
at which the objects
\mathfrak{r}_{\chi}^{n}
become Hermitian. We posit that
n
should also be related to the order of the quantum deformation parameter
q
, as a primitive root of unity.

Remark 6.6
.

Given the categorical
S^{4}
-formula for
\tilde{C}
, as argued for in
Remark
6.3
, we see that in order for for the Hopf links to be self-adjoint, the quantity
\overline{\mathfrak{r}}
must be isomorphic to
\mathfrak{r}^{T}
through a conjugation by invertible objects. Conversely, suppose we assume
\mathfrak{r}
is itself quasi-Hermitian, then the order
n
at which the Hopf links
\chi_{\cal D}
become trivializable can be deduced from
four times
the order of the antipode functor
\tilde{S}^{\circ 4n}\cong 1_{\tilde{C}}
.

\Diamond

We emphasize that the hypotheses of
Proposition
6.8
are sufficient conditions for the self-adjointedness of the Hopf links, but they may not be necessary. We now examine how these true Hopf links acquire additional relations among each other in the next section, depending on how framed
{\cal D}
is.

6.2.5
True Hopf links and the ribbon framing

Suppose
{\cal D}
is unframed as given in in
Definition
6.5
, which means in particular that
{\cal D}^{*}\cong\,^{*}{\cal D}
and (
4.4
) holds. From this, we immediately have the following.

Proposition 6.9
.

When
{\cal D}
is unframed, then

\alpha_{\cal D}\cong\bar{\beta}_{\cal D}\cong\bar{\alpha}_{\cal D}\cong\beta_{\cal D}.

Further, if
{\cal D}
were self-dual then there is only one true Hopf link on
{\cal D}
up to isomorphism.

Proof.

By
Remark
3.4
and the pivotality condition (
4.4
), we have

\operatorname{ev}^{\dagger}_{\cal D}=\operatorname{cev}_{{\cal D}^{*}}\cong\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D},\qquad\operatorname{cev}^{\dagger}_{\cal D}=\operatorname{ev}_{{\cal D}^{*}}\cong\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}.

Further, we have the following 2-isomorphisms

C_{{\cal D}^{*},\,^{*}{\cal D}}\cong C_{{\cal D}^{*},{\cal D}^{*}}\cong C_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},\,^{*}{\cal D}}\cong C_{\,{}^{*}{\cal D},{\cal D}^{*}},

(6.12)

whence the first part of the proposition follows.

Now if
{\cal D}
were self-dual, then not only do all of the true Hopf links on
{\cal D}
coincide, but they are also isomorphic to their duals,

\alpha_{\cal D}^{*}\cong\alpha_{{\cal D}^{*}}=\alpha_{\cal D}

through the 2-Hopf modifications.
∎

Similar to table
2
, we can also construct a table listing the distinct true Hopf links depending on how framed the object
{\cal D}
is.

{\cal D}
is…

fully-framed

unframed

self-dual

Hopf links

\chi_{\cal D},\bar{\chi}_{\cal D}

\chi^{*}_{\cal D},\,^{*}\bar{\chi}_{\cal D}

+ adjs.

\alpha_{\cal D},\alpha^{*}_{\cal D}
+ adjs.

\alpha_{\cal D}
+ adj.

Table 3:
A table listing the number of distinct true Hopf links up to 2-isomorphism depending on how framed
{\cal D}
is. Here,
\chi=\alpha,\beta
and by "+ adj." we mean that the adjoints of the Hopf links are also in general distinct, unless the hypothesis of
Proposition
6.8
holds.

Keep in mind that the self-adjointedness of the Hopf links are a priori independent of the framing. In particular, the tensor unit
{\cal I}
is
always
self-dual. Moreover, from
Proposition
3.2
we know that
c_{{\cal I},{\cal I}}\cong 1_{\cal I}
, hence the ribbon balancings and the Hopf links

\alpha_{\cal I}\cong 2\langle\mathfrak{Dim}({\cal I})\rangle 1_{{\cal I}},\qquad\vartheta_{\cal I}\cong\langle\mathfrak{Dim}({\cal I})\rangle\cdot 1_{\cal I}

are proportional to the trace of the 2-categorical dimension
\mathfrak{Dim}({\cal I})
defined in
Remark
4.2
.

7
Pivotality in the classical limit

Throughout the above, we have remarked repeatedly that full pivotality should be recovered in the classical limit. In the final section of this paper, we make this statement precise. In doing so, we will also see that being unframed is closely related to pivotality.

Theorem 7.1
.

In the classical limit,
\operatorname{2Rep}(\mathbb{U}_{q=1}\mathfrak{G};\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{id})
is pivotal in the sense of
[
DR18
]
.

Proof.

By
Proposition
3.5
, we just need to produce all of the C1-C8 conditions in Definition 2.2.4 of
[
DR18
]
. The conditions C2-C4 have already been demonstrated in §
3.3
, hence it suffices to check C1, C5-C8. We know from §
2.3
and
Remark
7
that, in the classical limit, the antipode
\tilde{S}
is unipotent, and hence gives an identification
{\cal D}\cong({\cal D}^{*})^{*}
as
\tilde{C}
-module categories. This settles C7.

Note the 2-gauge transformations satisfy
\Lambda_{\gamma_{\bar{e}}}=\Lambda_{\gamma_{e}}^{-1}
under under orientation reversal in the classical limit, whence

\Lambda_{\tilde{S}_{v}\gamma_{e}}=\Lambda_{\gamma_{\bar{e}}}=\Lambda_{\gamma_{e}}^{-1}=\Lambda_{\tilde{S}\gamma_{e}},

which tells us that the vertical and horizontal antipodes
\tilde{S}_{v},\tilde{S}
coincide on the edge parameters/1-morphisms
{\cal E}
. Since
\tilde{S}_{v}
determines the adjunction and
\tilde{S}
determines duality, the planar-pivotality of the folds imply that we have

\overline{\operatorname{cev}}_{\cal D}\cong\operatorname{ev}^{\dagger}_{\cal D},\qquad\overline{\operatorname{ev}}_{\cal D}\cong\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}^{\dagger};

however,
\overline{\operatorname{c/ev}}_{\cal D}\cong\operatorname{c/ev}_{{\cal D}^{*}}
whenever the dual is involutive, whence C5-C6 follow. From the fact that the braiding is trivial (ie. merely given by the flip functor), the 2-Drinfel’d transformations
\omega_{\cal D},\bar{\omega}_{\cal D}
are trivial as well, and in particular invertible. C8 then follows from
Proposition
6.1
.

Now it remains to recover C1, the swallowtail equations. We shall do this from the swallowking equations described in §
5.3.2
. Since the braiding is trivial, so is the writhing. The 2-morphism (
5.10
) simply takes the form
\upsilon_{\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D},\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D}}^{\dagger}\circ\operatorname{id}_{\text{flip}}\circ\upsilon_{\operatorname{cev}_{\cal D},\operatorname{ev}_{\cal D}}
. The unitarity of the interchangers then reduces the 2-morphism
\mathcal{K}_{\cal D}
, which we recall is obtained by attaching the left- and right-sides of the diamond (
5.11
) with (
5.10
), to
\operatorname{id}_{\text{flip}}
. The swallowking equations (
5.12
) then take the form

\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}\circ\operatorname{id}_{\text{flip}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{\cal D}^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}_{\text{flip}}.

This forces each of the swallowtails
\mathcal{S}_{\cal D},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\cal D}
to be trivial.
∎

It can be seen from the proof that every object in
\operatorname{2Rep}(\mathbb{U}_{q=1}\mathfrak{G};\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{id})
is
unframed
in the sense of
Definition
6.5
, whence (
4.4
) tells us that the object-level pairing convention is given by the pivotal one chosen in
[
DR18
]
. This result is consistent with the fact that
\operatorname{2Rep}(\mathbb{G})
for
finite
2-groups
\mathbb{G}
— which, like ordinary finite 1-groups, have no non-trivial deformation — are known to be pivotal
[
DR18
,
BBG23
]
. In fact, such 2-representation 2-categories are expected to be spherical, hence we also expect
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
to become spherical in the classical limit.

We conclude this paper by making the following remark. From
Remark
4.2
and table
2
, one can interpret the cause of the issue raised in Warning 2.2.5 of
[
DR18
]
as the fact that unframed objects are unable to tell barred and unbarred functors apart. As such, the data of being "unframed", namely the fixed chosen invertible 2-Drinfel’d modification which identifies
\vartheta
and
\bar{\vartheta}
, gives rise to a notion of "unframed equivalence" (ie. "pivotal adjoint equivalence" mentioned in Remark 2.3.9 of
[
DR18
]
) of objects preserving this data. In general, this notion relaxes to the centralizer subcategory
C_{\operatorname{End}({\cal D})}(\vartheta)
of the twists mentioned in
Remark
6.2
.

8
Conclusions

We have studied in this paper the notion of a "ribbon tensor 2-category" arising naturally out of the categorical quantum symmetries of the 4d 2-Chern-Simons theory on a lattice. Much of the properties and the coherence conditions were derived, but the author would like to emphasize that "ribbon tensor 2-categories" should exist in contexts much more general than just categorical quantum groups. In particular, such structures should at least have framing properties that leads to a "
SO(3)
-volutive" refinement of strict pivotality studied in
[
DR18
]
. It is the hope of the author to continue and use the structures examined here in order to construct interesting 4d TQFTs and invariants in the future.

Though, that is not to say that the only novelty of this paper is to give an example of such a ribbon 2-category. It also leads to very interesting implications in the study of 4d and 3d TQFTs. For instance, as we have noted in
Remark
6.7
and Table
1
, ribbon tensor 2-categories give rise to
\mathsf{Gray}
-categories with duals. As mentioned also in the introduction, such structures were shown to describe (locally) non-extended 3d defect TQFTs
[
CMS20
,
CRS20
,
Kop+22
,
CM23
]
, and serves as the natural foundation for their orbifold defect data. As such,
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{C};\tilde{R})
provides a way in which the 3d defect TQFTs can be parameterized, through different choices of Lie 2-groups, by the categorical quantum symmetries arising from the 4d 2-Chern-Simons TQFT.

This observation can also be understood as the manifestation of a 4d-3d topological bulk-boundary relation, as formulated in the setting of topological orders and quantum liquids in the series
[
KTZ20a
,
KZ22
,
KZ24
]
of papers. These papers relied heavily on the theory of the so-called
separable
n
-categories
and the condensation completion functor
[
GJ19
]
. The prevailing philosophy in the literature (see the above references, as well as eg.
[
KK12
,
Kon+20
]
) is that, given a modular tensor category describing a 3d topological order, its condensation completion describes a 4d topological bulk admitting it as boundary condition. We see that this perspective leads to a very interesting prospect for understanding the 4d-3d correspondence for the Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFT.

The algebraic Baez conjecture.

Take the modular tensor category
\operatorname{Rep}U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
underlying the Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFT
[
RT90
,
RT91
]
. Its condensation completion, or simply the pre-modular tensor 2-category
\operatorname{Mod}(\operatorname{Rep}U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
of its module categories, is believed to underlie the 4d Crane-Yetter TQFT
[
DR18
]
. Recent results
[
Haï25
]
have shown that the Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFT does indeed exist as a boundary condition of the Crane-Yetter TQFT.

On the other hand, starting from the classical theory, it can be shown
[
CL24
]
that the 3d Chern-Simons action with gauge group
G
lives on the boundary of the 4d 2-Chern-Simons action on the
inner automorphism 2-group

\operatorname{Inn}G=G\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}}G
. This is in fact also true
semi
classically in the BFV formalism
[
CP17
]
: the Chern-Simons pre-symplectic form also appears at the boundary of that of the 2-Chern-Simons theory.

Given the skein-theoretic quantization of Chern-Simons theory as the (Witten-)Reshetikhin-Turaev TQFT
[
Wit90
]
, it is therefore natural to ask the following question.

At the quantum level, how is the 2-Chern-Simons TQFT is related to the Crane-Yetter TQFT?

As mentioned in
[
Che25
]
, this question relates to a statement/conjecture made by Baez in
[
Bae96
]
, which presupposes that the 2-Chern-Simons theory on
\operatorname{Inn}SU(2)
is equivalent to the
SO(4)
Crane-Yetter TQFT.

The algebraic version of this statement is then a (pre-modular) equivalence

\operatorname{2Rep}(\mathbb{U}_{q}\operatorname{inn}\mathfrak{sl}(2))\simeq\operatorname{Mod}(\operatorname{Rep}(U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2})),

which given the results of this paper can be directly checked. This will be the subject of a future work.
